We wanted to tap into that and listen in on those conversations,” said Aja Whitaker-Moore, the deputy editor-in-chief of the Journal. “We’re taking a massive set of information…and telling you, from a statistical standpoint: ‘Here’s what people are talking about. Here are the questions they’re asking. Here are the places where those conversations are happening.'”
  > 
  > Other newsrooms have leaned on AI “visibility tools” in the past to support their reporting. That includes using large language models (LLMs) to transcribe and summarize “manosphere” podcasts, school board meetings, and local talk radio shows. But Doppler goes further.
  > 
  > Rather than using AI-generated summaries to generate leads or help beat reporters monitor online content more efficiently, the Journal uses Doppler to conduct large-scale AI analyses quickly — and publishes those insights for readers.
  > 
  > So far, analyses produced by Doppler have led to four major midterms stories in the Journal, with more expected to run before Election Day. For now, each story requires a custom treatment, with engineers and data journalists working alongside the D.C. politics team to refine the methodology, toggle on and off different features, and audit the results.
  > 
  > “The tool is only as good as the questions that you ask it,” said Whitaker-Moore. The Journal holds a weekly editorial meeting leading up to Election Day to workshop story angles and find new threads to explore.
  > 
  > @wallstreetjournal
  > 
  > A new in-house AI tool lets WSJ reporters analyze trends in online conversation to help understand the political mood of the country ahead of the midterm elections. A previous post paired a visual of TikToks from across the internet with a headline that identified them as conservative podcasters.
  > 
  > ♬ original sound – The Wall Street Journal
  > 
  > A story published on October 8 analyzed over 30,000 hours of podcasts and documented how, in recent weeks, right-leaning podcasters had started discussing “affordability” as often as left-leaning podcasters, despite President Trump calling the affordability crisis a “hoax.”
  > 
  > Another story published on October 1 traced anti-AI sentiment, including opposition to Flock surveillance cameras, by analyzing more than four million TikTok videos and thousands of hours of podcasts. The Journal found that posters who shared anti-Flock sentiments disagreed on other political positions about 12% of the time, three times the average rate across other political topics tracked — putting the issue “at the top of the list of positions that unite creators who are otherwise at odds.”
  > 
  > For a story published on October 15, the Journal pulled content about 67 Democratic politicians from its Doppler database. The analysis showed that the Democratic politician currently receiving the most favorable commentary is Angie Nixon, the progressive Senate nominee in Florida. About 70% of TikTok videos posted about her in recent weeks were favorable. She was followed by Georgia Senator Jon Ossoff at 54%. Meanwhile, Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer ranked the lowest in the analysis; just 3% of videos posted about him were favorable.
  > 
  > [img wide]Courtesy of The Wall Street Journal[/img]
  > 
  > Whitaker-Moore said the idea for Doppler was inspired by conversations with Washington bureau chief Damian Paletta and politics editor Christopher Matthews. They thought the coverage of the 2024 U.S. election missed important undercurrents in public opinion that never surfaced in polling. And though reporters acknowledged the power of podcasts in that election cycle, there weren’t many standardized ways to capture what was being said across those channels.
  > 
  > Coming out of those conversations, Whitaker-Moore posed a question to Rob Barry, who leads the Journal’s data journalism team: “Can you build something that can eat the internet and then feed it back to us?”
  > 
  > The Journal had already built its own podcast transcription and summarization tool, Orca. (The name refers to the tight-knit pods in which orcas travel.) Orca had collected countless hours of audio and YouTube videos, not just of political podcasts but from genres like health and wellness.
  > 
  > The Journal’s experience with TikTok-based investigations made the social media platform’s videos another natural source of information for Doppler. It’s nearly impossible to create a comprehensive database of TikTok videos, or even a truly random sample. Instead, Barry’s team used a monitoring tool that relies on TikTok’s own recommendation algorithms to identify the accounts it should track.
  > 
  > “Every day we’re adding tons of data across all the different platforms that we’re looking at,” Barry told me. “The database is growing in the background constantly.”
  > 
  > To make sense of that data, Doppler has a tech stack that includes different types of AI models, including LLMs. The model used depends on the specific reporting question.
  > 
  > Embedding models have been central to some of stories published so far. These AI models convert text or images into vectors — lists of numbers that capture the meaning of each piece of content. By vectorizing the content, Doppler can map out the ideas in a dataset and see where they naturally cluster together.
  > 
  > The Journal’s data journalism team has used vectorization in the past, including in its Pulitzer Prize–winning story that mapped out Elon Musk’s entire history of tweets. In Doppler, vectorization facilitates a kind of internet radar, helping reporters identify where patterns are forming across its social media and podcasting database.
  > 
  > For now, the Journal’s political reporters can’t just start using Doppler on their own. The exact methodology has been tailored to each story published, with the help of engineers.
  > 
  > “There’s a lot of human judgment in this. My team would not have aimed for a tool that seeks to just produce stuff that we then put directly on the internet,” Barry said. “You still need that human intermediary, that human to apply the judgment and to ask the question.”
  > 
  > The Journal is attaching a short methodology to every story assisted by Doppler. It makes clear that AI was used to produce the piece, briefly explains how the analysis came together, and notes some of the tool’s limitations.
  > 
  > Despite manual spot checks of data classifications and other safeguards to make sure the tool’s outputs are accurate, Barry said, “there’s almost always going to be some level of error and uncertainty and blur around the edges.” The disclosures are one way to “give readers a sense for what that level of issue is.”
  > 
  > Looking ahead, the Journal is considering bringing Doppler to other desks in the newsroom. The data being collected is not inherently political, and it could be used to help answer a host of other reporting questions, drawing on insights scattered across the internet.
  > 
  > “We definitely think that [Doppler News AI] can live beyond the midterms and live beyond elections,” Whitaker-Moore said.

### 9. Two out of five Americans regularly get their news on Facebook
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/two-out-of-five-americans-are-regularly-getting-their-news-on-facebook/
- 发布时间：Tue, 20 Oct 2026 19:07:52 +0000
- 全文：
  > Despite what my Facebook feed looks like, the platform’s not just for AI fruit videos and birthday posts.
  > 
  > According to data from the Pew Research Center published Monday, 40% of adults in the United States regularly get their news from Facebook.
  > 
  > That’s up from 36% in 2020, signaling the continued stability of the platform as a news source — a trend that the Reuters Institute for the Study of Journalism observed in its Digital News Report this year. As we reported in June, people around the world are increasingly abandoning news websites for social media.
  > 
  > The Center surveyed 5,600 U.S. adults in late August as part of its American Trends Panel, and asked respondents several questions about their news and social media habits. Pew did not give respondents a definition of “news,” instead letting them use their own understanding of the term when they filled out the survey. More than half (59%) said that they get news from social media “often or sometimes,” but only 19% actually prefer social media as a place to get news.
  > 
  > Of Facebook news consumers, 33% are men and 49% are between the ages of 30 and 49. The platform is most popular for women (47%), Hispanic Americans (51%), Americans with a high school diploma or less (46%), and Republicans (43%).
  > 
  > Regular news consumption among American users has increased on nine of the 12 social media platforms that Pew studied, including YouTube, Instagram, TikTok, X (Twitter), Reddit, Nextdoor, LinkedIn, and WhatsApp. News consumption has either dropped or stayed the same on Rumble, Truth Social, and Bluesky.
  > 
  > The second-most regularly used platform among American adults is YouTube (34%), followed by Instagram (23%), TikTok (20%), and X (10%). YouTube news consumers are more likely to be male (41%), between ages 18 and 29 (39%), and identify as Black (47%) or Asian (53%). Republicans (35%) and Democrats (36%) are equal YouTube news consumers.
  > 
  > The most regularly used platform for news among young adults is Instagram (43%), followed by Facebook (40%), TikTok and YouTube (both 39%), and X and Reddit (both 18%). The Center found that 20% of U.S. adults now regularly get their news on TikTok, up from 3% in 2020. Of TikTok users, 53% reported getting news regularly on the platform. Nextdoor users are also increasingly turning to the neighborhood forum app for information. In 2026, 45% of Nextdoor users reported regularly getting news from the app, up from 23% in 2022.
  > 
  > Read the full report here.

### 10. When hackers become sources: Inside the new craft of reporting on hacked data
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/when-hackers-become-sources-inside-the-new-craft-of-reporting-on-hacked-data/
- 发布时间：Tue, 20 Oct 2026 15:39:06 +0000
- 全文：
  > Some consequential reporting of recent years has emerged thanks to what is normally defined as a “hack-and-leak,” a specific kind of cyberattack that results in the release of the information obtained by hackers. When Anonymous and allied collectives breached corporations and state agencies during the hacktivist wave of the 2010s, for instance, they turned stolen data into front-page news.
  > 
  > Reporters covering the invasion of Ukraine have frequently worked with documents pulled from Russian institutions by hackers of different allegiances. Hacktivists have breached surveillance vendors and spyware firms, granting journalists direct and exclusive access to the internal files of companies that sell to repressive governments. Ransomware gangs, cybercriminal groups, and state-affiliated hackers deposit fresh troves of hacked data on the open internet, or route it to the public through intermediaries like Distributed Denial of Secrets.
  > 
  > How are journalists responding to these reporting scenarios? For my research, recently published in Journalism Practice, I interviewed 10 journalists across the U.S. and Europe who work or have worked with hacked data. Some of them specialize in the coverage of cyber issues; others mainly focus on current affairs and global conflict. These journalists work for a variety of news outlets, from investigative reporting newsrooms to general interest outlets and technology brands, and have engaged with hacker sources at various levels.
  > 
  > The journalists I spoke with paint a picture of an emerging craft, rather than a settled method. Here’s some of what I found.
  > 
  > There are no fixed guidelines or established and shared practices on how to handle and report on hacked material in news reporting. Instead, the methods are being developed by practitioners on a case by case basis. One reporter who works for an investigative newsroom told me:
  > 
  > On one hand, you have the traditional investigative journalists digging through large datasets from major leaks, who approach the material with the classic reporting toolkit, corroboration, interviews, contextualizing the data…On the other hand, there’s this newer, more technical niche where people work with constantly emerging, often smaller-scale data exposures, like scraping dark web dumps, pastebins, or unsecured cloud buckets, and turning those into fast-paced stories about breaches, vulnerabilities, and digital privacy. It’s less about deep-dive investigation and more about monitoring and exposing ongoing data exposures in real time.
  > 
  > Hackers themselves are becoming increasingly strategic about their relationships with the press. One reporter following a specific cyber beat emphasized this:
  > 
  > “I’d say that [hackers] of all kinds love to flex and get credit. A lot of them don’t, but many absolutely do. I’m not sure if the desire to flex has increased, but you’ve definitely seen the maturity of some hackers in how they approach the media. It’s not just about posting things to Twitter like it might have been before.
  > 
  > That maturity means developing awareness of how a story will be framed and disseminated and calculating which journalist to approach. Hackers now may select their outlets of choice, matching a leak to the reporter who they think is capable of understanding the story and carrying it further.
  > 
  > There are different kinds of hackers, wearing different and/or multiple hats. Interviewees drew distinctions between financially motivated cybercriminals, whose leaks are often equivalent to extortion by other means, and state-affiliated actors, who may also pose as independent hacktivists to launder politically motivated leaks into credible coverage.
  > 
  > At the same time, some hackers or hacker collectives — like Phineas Fisher, known for his activist remit against surveillance companies — may act for political and public interest–oriented reasons, reaching out to journalists to expose wrongdoing, unethical issues, or crimes. Notoriously, though, hacking is not a field in which hats are worn straightforwardly: Identities, definitions and agendas, including mere trolling, may blur into one another.
  > 
  > Hacked material needs to be handled with a great deal of skepticism. When working with hacked sources, one journalist said, it’s a matter of “treating every dataset as if it has chlamydia.” But the verification techniques the journalists said they use — such as confirming that an email address exists in a company’s records, cross-referencing with known facts, or examining a file for signs of tampering — are fairly conventional. This type of reporting requires more disclosures, especially when a source’s identity can’t be established. One journalist working in this area said:
  > 
  > I think you should be very transparent about that in your reporting. You should say something like, “We don’t know who the source is; it could be the GRU [Russian Federation] or whoever, but we have confirmed that this data is real and this is what it shows.”
  > 
  > Declared openly, the uncertainty may become a meaningful part of the story itself.
  > 
  > Source protection is further complicated when it comes to hackers. Some interviewees extend the full protection they would give any source to hackers, refusing to assist law enforcement even if the source is a self-declared criminal. Others prefer to know as little as possible about their sources, framing the choice as harm reduction for both parties. As one journalist in my sample put it:
  > 
  > The less I know about these things, the harder it’s going to be for me to burn my source or to publish something that inadvertently turns out to be harmful to my source.
  > 
  > Reporters working exclusively with material already circulating online in cybercrime forums or other repositories on the web treat source protection as beside the point, because the origins of the information cannot be traced and because the material is in effect “out there.”
  > 
  > Beneath all of this sits the finding with the widest implications: Ethical decisions in this domain resist strict codification. No general rule can be written in advance, the reporters I spoke with suggested, and judgment in the individual case is the only workable guide. Is publishing hacked material justified? It depends on the specifics of the case at hand, one reporter told me.
  > 
  > I kind of think it all depends on what you’re exposing. Maybe it’s generally wrong to break into people’s computers and steal data, but if you’re exposing serious crimes, then I think it can be justified.
  > 
  > Interviewees described practical wisdom under pressure: the ability to read a specific situation and act appropriately n it, without having the opportunity to refer to an established code or checklist.
  > 
  > Newsrooms, meanwhile, may need to develop verification standards suited to information of illicit origin, transparency norms that treat uncertainty as something to disclose rather than paper over, and a shared language for the ethical judgments that journalists currently make case by case on their own.
  > 
  > Philip Di Salvo is a senior researcher and lecturer at the Institute for Media and Communications Management at the University of St. Gallen, Switzerland.
  > 
  > Adobe Stock

### 11. “Convenings” for CEOs will help Semafor reach a second profitable year
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/convenings-for-ceos-will-help-semafor-reach-a-second-profitable-year/
- 发布时间：Mon, 19 Oct 2026 17:06:55 +0000
- 全文：
  > Semafor expects to have its second profitable year, thanks to a growing events, er, convening business, cofounders Justin Smith and Ben Smith announced.
  > 
  > The news company, which launched four years ago, had its first profitable year last year on $40 million in revenue. In a note published Sunday, Smith and Smith said they expect to close the year “substantially bigger” in “revenue and profitability and reach.”
  > 
  > About half of the company’s revenue comes from live events, which Semafor refers to as “convenings.” A two-day convening in November, for example, will cover “Silicon Valley & The World.” The co-chairs include names like OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, and Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella.
  > 
  > In their note, the cofounders wrote that “Semafor is starting to emerge clearly as the thing that we hoped it would become.”
  > 
  > “We launched with the insight that a leadership audience — CEOs and political leaders alike — was, paradoxically, the most desperate for efficient, fair, and sophisticated news,” they continued.
  > 
  > It’s a stretch to say CEOs and political leaders are “desperate” for news. Existing news consumers tend to be older, wealthier, more white collar, and more politically engaged than those who don’t consume news. The genuinely underserved news audiences are elsewhere. Journalism for “decision makers” is one of the most crowded markets in news. Outlets like Bloomberg, Politico Pro, Financial Times, Punchbowl News, Axios Pro, and The Information operate in a similar space.
  > 
  > But readers aren’t the primary financial supporters of Semafor’s business. That’d be the corporate sponsors paying for access to its audience through ads and events. Although the annual update says the company has 1.2 million subscriptions, those remain free subscriptions for now.
  > 
  > By building a profitable and growing news business, Semafor has done something exceedingly rare. (It now has journalists on the ground in 11 countries and plans to launch in Asia in 2027.) Editor-in-chief Ben Smith explained some of his thinking on an episode of Decoder this summer.
  > 
  > In my memory, we always thought at some point we’d do subscriptions, but in my experience you just cannot be ideological about revenue. It’s hard enough to build a business in news without getting all hung up on the values of the different revenue streams.
  > 
  > Advertising-based publishers will tell you that democracy can only survive in a world where there aren’t paywalls. Subscription publishers will tell you that advertising is corrupting. At some point you realize everyone’s just talking about their book and that sometimes they’re already in a tough business and they’re talking themselves out of incremental opportunities and really successful media companies.
  > 
  > It’s “impossible” for publishers to compete with Google and Meta in the scaled advertising business, Smith told Decoder. He described Semafor as “an anti-scale business.”
  > 
  > If you’re reaching people in a very focused way in various particular environments — in person, in email, in video — that’s a case you can really credibly make to advertisers. We’ve built a big advertising and related convening business that way, and that’s one answer. It’s not the only answer, but it is one.
  > 
  > The note is here.

### 12. Legal experts warn Meta’s new terms still grant broad scraping permissions, despite language changes
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/legal-experts-warn-metas-new-terms-still-grant-broad-scraping-permissions-despite-language-changes/
- 发布时间：Sat, 17 Oct 2026 18:30:46 +0000
- 全文：
  > Meta has removed the word “train” from its upcoming terms of service for business accounts. The company revised its terms after Nieman Lab’s story on Thursday exposed that the new terms would permit broad scraping of websites “associated with” business accounts, and possibly allow Meta to use that scraped content to train its AI models.
  > 
  > In its revision, published Friday afternoon, Meta removed the word “train” from a list of allowed use cases and limited those uses to “advertising and business products,” rather than all Meta products. But the revised terms will still allow for broad scraping of websites, potentially superseding robots.txt files, according to legal experts.
  > 
  > On Saturday, a Meta spokesperson said the company revised the terms because “it wasn’t clear in the initial consolidation of terms that robots.txt preferences are respected. News sites, like all other content owners, still have the ability to disable the crawler via robots.txt flags. That has been the case, and it has not changed.”
  > 
  > Meta has not included any specific language about respecting robots.txt preferences in its revised terms of service. The new terms are slated to go into effect on October 30. There is no option for business accounts to opt out of the new terms before then.
  > 
  > “I think publishers should remain concerned,” said Matt Topic, a media and intellectual property attorney at Loevy & Loevy. “The revised version of the new terms still allows Meta to use that scraped content to develop any ‘business product.’ That doesn’t seem to be a defined term, and presumably Meta is developing AI business products. I think ‘develop’ is broad enough to include ‘train.'”
  > 
  > Meta currently lists examples of its business products as “ads, Meta Business Tools and Meta Business Suite.”
  > 
  > Virtually every news organization has at least one Meta business account. The accounts let companies manage Facebook pages, Threads accounts, Instagram profiles, and WhatsApp channels from one place.
  > 
  > As of publication time, the section reads (with changes in bold):
  > 
  > At least three journalism organizations have publicly raised concerns about the forthcoming terms as a result of Nieman Lab’s reporting.
  > 
  > Prior to the update on Friday, the European Publishers Council announced that it would formally petition the E.U.’s telecommunications regulatory authorities and its executive body, the European Commission, to “scrutinize these terms urgently, including their implications for copyright, contractual fairness and abuse of market power.”
  > 
  > The United Kingdom News Media Association, the trade organization representing British media, issued a statement on Friday calling for government officials to intervene.
  > 
  > “This shameless landgrab attempt from Meta to scrape anything and everything from news publishers and business websites to train their AI products is the worst kind of arrogant tech platform bullying,” said chief executive Theo Bamber. “We urgently need Ministers and regulators to stand up for British creators and the business community by calling out this outrageous behaviour and preventing Meta from setting this dreadful precedent.”
  > 
  > The News/Media Alliance — the largest news publishing trade association in the United States — denounced the terms, with president and CEO Danielle Coffey calling the update “outrageous and probably illegal.”
  > 
  > “The company’s decision to withhold access to its audience unless businesses allow Meta to scrape content from their websites is an appalling, bad-faith policy, and a clear abuse of the company’s market power,” Coffey said. “It cannot be allowed to be implemented on October 30.”
  > 
  > This story has been updated to include comments from Meta.
  > 
  > Are you considering changing how you use Meta-owned platforms? Let us know: staff@niemanlab.org.

### 13. Meta’s new commercial terms will allow the scraping of news sites, legal experts say
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/metas-new-commercial-terms-will-allow-the-scraping-of-news-sites-legal-experts-say/
- 发布时间：Thu, 15 Oct 2026 16:59:54 +0000
- 全文：
  > Meta can use web scrapers to crawl and collect content from websites “associated with” business accounts on its platforms, according to its new commercial terms of use.
  > 
  > The update, set to go into effect on October 30, will include news publishers with business accounts. These accounts allow companies or public figures to manage Facebook pages, Messenger accounts, Instagram profiles, or WhatsApp channels from one place. While journalism isn’t the only industry that will be affected by this update, virtually every news organization has at least one Meta business account, including Nieman Lab.
  > 
  > The new terms broadly cover business accounts that manage pages and groups, run advertisements, push marketing messages, and use the platform’s business account dashboard. As written, the new terms could be used to argue that Meta can scrape content from news sites and use that content to train the company’s AI products, according to three legal experts who reviewed the terms at Nieman Lab’s request.
  > 
  > “This is a remarkable provision to bury in [the] terms of service, given that this would give Meta access to material that would likely cost many millions of dollars in a licensing market,” said Matt Topic, a media and intellectual property attorney at Loevy & Loevy. “It’s not clear whether a court would enforce this, but publishers should assume that it would be enforceable.”
  > 
  > In early October, Meta alerted users to the update in a blog post and on the Meta Business Suite. The updated terms explicitly state that Meta can use the content it collects from associated websites to “train” Meta products. Elsewhere in its terms of service, the company defines “AI at Meta” as one of those products.
  > 
  > Here’s the new language about scraping in full:
  > 
  > You authorize us (or any entity acting on our behalf) to use automated software (such as web scrapers, bots, robots, spiders, crawlers, and user agents) and create test credentials to access, index, cache, analyze, collect, retrieve, or crawl the domains, web pages, or URLs (and any content available through them) that are associated with your products or services, or that are made available through your or users’ use of Meta Company Products and services, and to periodically pull updates from such domains, web pages, or URLs. Meta may use any content from such domains, web pages, or URLs to provide, develop, improve, research, train, and maintain the integrity of, the Meta Company Products, services, and features provided under these Commercial Terms and any other applicable supplemental terms.
  > 
  > “For news publishers, this clearly represents permission to Meta to train AI on any content from their sites and possibly to deploy some of that content, even in competition with the publishers themselves,” said James Grimmelmann, a professor of digital and information law at Cornell Law School. “On the broadest possible interpretation, this would be used to develop a comprehensive archive of all content that Meta would then serve as it wanted to directly or through AI.”
  > 
  > Currently, there is no option for business accounts to opt out of these new terms ahead of October 30. The update reads, “If you do not agree to the updated terms, please stop all access or use of our Products for business or commercial purposes.”
  > 
  > In short, “publishers either have to trust that Meta doesn’t mean the full extent of what these terms say, or they have to stop [using business accounts],” said Grimmelmann.
  > 
  > Meta declined to answer a list of questions about these updated terms. A public affairs spokesperson at the company said in an email that language around crawling was previously scattered across different terms of use, including terms about using generative AI in ads, and this update consolidates them in one place. He also said that the new terms will not change how Meta crawls publishers in the future, and that “content owners can still manage how their content is accessed by using robots.txt.”
  > 
  > On its developer page, Meta currently has instructions for how to block its crawlers using robots.txt, a file that tells bots which parts of a website they can and cannot access.
  > 
  > However, legal experts said the new language creates an opening for Meta to argue its commercial terms supersede other blocking instructions. The update also states that Meta can create “test credentials” in order to scrape websites associated with business accounts. Grimmelmann said this would technically allow Meta to “sign up as a fake user” to bypass registration walls on a publisher’s site to access content. Meta declined to answer Nieman Lab’s questions about whether the “test credentials” would bypass paywalls or other paid content.
  > 
  > Any attempt to train Meta’s AI products with content collected under these new terms would still hinge on unsettled legal questions about AI training and fair use, legal experts said. Several lawsuits between publishers and AI developers are currently winding their way through federal courts. That includes a class-action suit filed in May by five large book publishers, alleging Meta pirated millions of books in order to train its Llama large language models (LLMs).
  > 
  > Meta’s decision to tether access to its business products to broad scraping permissions echoes a concept in antitrust law called “tying,” according to Benjamin Sobel, a law professor at the University of Wisconsin. “If you want to use the Meta products that require acceptance of the commercial terms, you have to also acquiesce to the scraping and crawling permissions exacted in those terms.”
  > 
  > Sobel compared the move to Google’s recent policies around its AI search products. In order to appear in Google’s traditional search results, publishers previously had to consent to their content appearing in AI Overviews and AI Mode. As of August 2026, all websites now have the option to opt out.
  > 
  > Meta’s new commercial terms come on the heels of the company’s rollout of its paid subscription service, Meta One. The cash-for-links program restricts the number of external links that some accounts on Facebook and Instagram can post without subscribing. Put another way, Meta has begun charging users to post links to the same content that it will be permitted to crawl for free. Earlier this month, Press Gazette reported that some news publishers are currently exempt from the link limit. Meta declined to answer Nieman Lab’s question about the service.
  > 
  > Nieman Lab first learned about Meta’s new commercial terms through a reader tip. Stephen Beale, the founder and editor of The Steampunk Explorer, got in touch when he saw a notice of the upcoming change in his Meta Business Suite dashboard.
  > 
  > “Traditionally, you have this idea that Meta might be using content [posted] on Facebook or Instagram for training,” Beale told Nieman Lab. “But this was different because they’re saying that [since you are] a business user of Facebook, they have the right to go out and grab information from basically wherever you have a presence.”
  > 
  > Beale has long depended on Facebook as a way to connect with his audience and refer traffic to his site. Fans of steampunk — a retrofuturistic subgenre of science fiction — skew older, he said, and the community is “totally entrenched on Facebook.” For many, their only interactions with the community happen on Facebook or at in-person events that they learned about on Facebook.
  > 
  > Beale said he’s seriously considering shutting down his accounts before October 30.
  > 
  > “I know my site has been scraped to death,” he said. “But it just seems like such an arrogant, in-your-face move on [Meta’s] part… It just makes me angry and I don’t want them to get away with it.”
  > 
  > Are you considering changing how you use Meta-owned platforms? Let us know at staff@niemanlab.org.
  > 
  > Correction: This story has been updated to reflect Google’s current AI search policies.

### 14. A new report argues newsrooms need to do more experiments — like, the kind with randomization and results
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/a-new-report-argues-newsrooms-need-to-do-more-experiments-like-the-kind-with-randomization-and-results/
- 发布时间：Thu, 15 Oct 2026 16:47:21 +0000
- 全文：
  > Mississippi Today had a question. Did the stories around its Pulitzer Prize-winning investigative reporting affect the way those investigations were received?
  > 
  > It’s the kind of question local newsrooms can’t easily test on their own. But researchers from Local NExT Lab at Syracuse University could.
  > 
  > In a national survey, 1,768 adults were shown the same investigation surrounded by a different mix of stories. In one, the investigation was shown alongside positive community stories. (Think: “How a local fossil park changed science history.”) In another, the investigation was featured alongside other investigative stories.
  > 
  > The investigation was rated more accurate, important, and relevant when it was surrounded by community-focused stories, not other investigative stories. (Audience reaction to a third story mix, where the investigation was surrounded by stories about legislation, was not consistent.)
  > 
  > The result was described in the first report from the Local NExT Lab. Joshua Darr, director of the lab and associate professor at Syracuse University, believes that although dashboards and engagement analytics are “very important tools” for newsrooms, they aren’t enough.
  > 
  > “Engagement data can’t tell you what caused audiences to engage, settle a hunch, or isolate your contribution,” the report reads. “Randomization can.”
  > 
  > “The key word there is ’caused,'” Darr added in an email. “Without randomization, you can tell when engagement is up or down or if a particular story is doing well, but it’s hard to know exactly why that is happening.”
  > 
  > At Nieman Lab, we often hear from both sides of academic-practitioner partnerships. Researchers wonder why newsrooms don’t pay attention to their findings or fail to respond to their overtures about experiments. Publishers, editors, and journalists, meanwhile, tell me they don’t have time to read dense and jargon-filled academic papers. They can be annoyed by requests to spend time or resources they don’t have. And it’s not always fun to be someone else’s test subject.
  > 
  > When partnering with Local NExT Lab, Darr emphasized, the local newsrooms don’t have to commit money or manpower. They agree to two or three conversations. The researchers listen and then design, field, and analyze the experiments. In return, the newsrooms get their ideas tested and causal evidence.
  > 
  > In another experiment, the Local NExt Lab found that Instagram Reels including reporters’ faces drove the most interest and engagement, but when it came to clicking “follow,” profiles that looked like traditional news brands won out. Respondents chose to follow newsrooms (in this case New Orleans Public Radio) more often than reporter-named accounts.
  > 
  > The first batch of tests was funded by the W.K. Kellogg Foundation. Additional support from the American Journalism Project will allow for further tests on a larger cohort next year. The full report is here.
  > 
  > Adobe Stock

### 15. Google’s News AI pilot expands to over 400 publications, including U.S. local news outlets
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/googles-news-ai-pilot-expands-to-over-400-publications-including-u-s-local-news-outlets/
- 发布时间：Thu, 15 Oct 2026 16:01:46 +0000
- 全文：
  > In December, Google launched its News AI pilot program, which licenses content from select publishers and uses it to test new AI features in Google News. On Thursday, the company announced that the program has added new partnerships, bringing the total number of publications in the pilot to more than 400 worldwide.
  > 
  > At launch, the program partnered with international publishers like The Guardian, El País, The Times of India, and The Washington Post. More publications are now coming on board, including CNN, The Financial Times, Axios, and Forbes.
  > 
  > As part of the expansion, the program has also added local news publishers across the U.S., including The Post and Courier in Charleston, South Carolina; Oil City News in Casper, Wyoming; the Illinois- and Iowa-based newspaper chain Shaw Media; and the nonprofit newsroom The Texas Tribune.
  > 
  > “People’s information consumption habits are changing, and we want to make sure publishers of all sizes, local newsrooms included, can navigate these shifts,” Brianna Puccini Duff, a spokesperson for Google, said in a statement to Nieman Lab.
  > 
  > Google declined to comment on the exact terms of the deals. However, Duff clarified that the News AI pilot does not operate on a “pay-per-value” model, meaning publishers are not compensated based on how much their content contributes to an AI-generated output. (The pay-per-value model is part of a parallel program that pays publishers for content surfaced in AI Overviews, AI Mode, and Gemini, according to a recent report by Digiday.)
  > 
  > With the News AI pilot, publishers are instead paid to provide content that appears in experimental AI-powered features. That includes “story previews” — in-depth, AI-generated article summaries of recent stories that may show up on a publisher’s Google News page. Here’s a Washington Post example, with a disclaimer noting the copy is AI-generated and “may include mistakes.”
  > 
  > An AI-generated “story preview” on the Google News page for The Washington Post
  > 
  > Another test feature is “audio briefing,” a customizable, AI-generated podcast in the Google News app that stitches together daily headlines. Users can select the topics they’re most interested in hearing about, including “World,” “Gaming,” or “Sports” news. The feature includes citations that link to articles mentioned in the briefing.

### 16. Two creators went to NYU’s business school, then decided to make a show about the news
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/two-creators-went-to-nyus-business-school-then-decided-to-make-a-show-about-the-news/
- 发布时间：Wed, 14 Oct 2026 19:43:55 +0000
- 全文：
  > The data is in: News creators and influencers are a major source of news for Americans, especially people under 30. This is the latest edition of Creators of Record, an occasional series of interviews with popular creators about how they do their jobs.
  > 
  > On a September afternoon in Manhattan’s Union Square neighborhood, Shreyas Sinha is sitting on a toilet, wearing a blue suit. Looking into the camera, he asks, “Why is it that everything I’ve been eating this year is giving me diarrhea?”
  > 
  > Sinha is about to link the 2026 cyclospora outbreaks to decades of problems at the Food and Drug Administration. “I’m only allowed to make three diarrhea jokes in this video,” Sinha says. “I live in tyranny.”
  > 
  > The toilet video is one of the tamer stunts that Sinha and his co-creator, Gaurav Srivatchan, have pulled for their YouTube channel, A Show About The News, a weekly deep dive into major business and politics stories (tagline: “Critics are calling it the worst alternative to mainstream media, and those are just the critics I’m related to.”)
  > 
  > In a video about why young people can’t afford to buy homes, Sinha pops out of a cardboard box on the street and takes former New York City mayor Eric Adams to a studio apartment filled with men. Other recent videos include a 45-minute interview on the street with former White House communications director Anthony Scaramucci, an explainer on prediction market ads, and a rap music video on why retirement investments are safer than get-rich-quick schemes.
  > 
  > Sinha, 25, and Srivatchan, 22, met in 2023 when they were students at New York University’s Stern School of Business. They teamed up to make short vertical explainer videos, but those didn’t take off as much as they’d hoped. In 2024, Sinha quit his finance job to pursue A Show About The News full-time, and they transitioned to creating long-form deep dives with a dash of their own humor.
  > 
  > In 2025, Sinha and Srivatchan signed a contract to make the show as a franchise of Morning Brew. (They declined to share the specifics of the partnership.) Sinha anchors most videos, with Srivatchan directing, editing, and producing. A Show About The News has 97,000 YouTube subscribers and 84,000 Instagram followers.
  > 
  > I chatted with Sinha and Srivatchan about how they launched their media careers, how they use humor to explain the news, and what they think business opportunities are for creators today. Our conversation has been edited for length and clarity.
  > 
  > Tameez: Why did you start creating news videos?
  > 
  > Sinha: Gaurav and I started making videos together during the spring 2023 semester, when I was a senior at NYU and he was a freshman. I had been doing a newsletter for a while, I had been doing stand-up comedy for a few years, and I was interning at CNN. It seemed like everything was [pointing to], “Hey, you should start making short-form videos.” And then Gaurav was like, “Hey, I’m actually really good at editing videos.”
  > 
  > We were not really thinking of it as a YouTube channel, but as making short-form stories. At the time, we were called Fifty50 Media. We were all about trying to distill the world into bipartisan perspectives. We eventually started focusing more on deeper-dive stories. We renamed ourselves to A Show About the News.
  > 
  > It’s very easy to put out a political opinion that goes viral, but we really wanted to focus on good stories. Eventually, we started making videos for Morning Brew, and we joined Morning Brew full-time as a franchise in August 2025.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: We realized that building a loyal audience off shorts wasn’t feasible in the long-term. And we constantly [faced] the challenge of bringing in nuance. That’s one of the reasons we started working on long-form videos. We try to bring in the political economy lens from our college education and highlight the underlying principles [of] today’s headlines.
  > 
  > Sinha: To be honest, we weren’t successful in short-form. We had made 50 or 60 shorts…
  > 
  > Srivatchan: Some of them would pop off, get a million views. But the way people consume on the internet is very different from the way people consume when they go to a theater, [where] they’re paying for an experience and they’re going to watch a specific thing.
  > 
  > YouTube and social media are like a theater where you can leave and go watch another movie at any time. That’s the challenge of making videos on the internet.
  > 
  > Sinha: After our first video that got a million views, we were like, “Oh shit, this is it.” And it wasn’t. We added followers, but it wasn’t like we were going to get 100,000 people to come back in the next short. We were competing against the new Frozen movie and The Office and Mr. Beast exploding dynamite in his backyard.
  > 
  > Tameez: Tell me more about how the Morning Brew deal happened.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: I was in London, studying abroad. I slapped a bunch of logos as watermarks on our old videos [and sent them as] demo reels to news outlets.
  > 
  > Sinha: We literally got zero responses…then an opportunity came to make a video for [Morning Brew] and it was a great experience. We made another and another, and then they were like, “You guys should just do this for us.”
  > 
  > Srivatchan: Morning Brew provided us access to this office. It’s great to not meet under a lofted bed in Shreyas’ apartment.
  > 
  > Sinha: They’re an excellent partner [because] they believe in allowing editorial control to be with the person who makes the video. We’re not going into meetings asking, can we do this topic next week? It’s a very high-trust environment, which, from talking to other people who work at other media companies, is extremely rare.
  > 
  > Tameez: You guys both left careers in finance to do this full-time.
  > 
  > Sinha: It was an economically stupid decision, but I knew that if I failed now, I would be young enough to start again. It’s easy to have the golden handcuffs of waiting until the right time, but there is never a right time.
  > 
  > It was taking a bet on myself and someone who I really trusted. Still, our videos were getting 12 views at that time; it wasn’t as if something took off and it was like, “Okay, this can pay our bills.”
  > 
  > Srivatchan: I had the advantage of being younger. [Shreyas left] a full-time job. I did my summer internship in consulting and got my return offer, but it was a lot less of a risk for me, personally. It obviously helped that we had some backing from Morning Brew.
  > 
  > Tameez: Why is making A Show About the News important now? How does it fit into the larger information ecosystem?
  > 
  > Sinha: What we do, in the context of where we went to school, is the equivalent of becoming ballerinas. All of my friends are investment bankers in private equity or in consulting. That is the tried and true method [for NYU Stern graduates]. If you tell people, “Hey, I’m not recruiting for banking, I’m thinking of doing consulting,” they look at you like you just said you want to be an art major. When we said we were going to build a media company, it was truly mind-boggling for people.
  > 
  > I loved what Fareed Zakaria did, what Ezra Klein did, but my favorite people when I was a kid were Jon Stewart and John Oliver, Hasan Minhaj and Seth Meyers and Conan O’Brien and Nathan Fielder. I knew I wasn’t going to be a great stand-up comedian, I wasn’t going to be a great writer, but maybe I could combine those to make a job out of it.
  > 
  > It’s easy to pat ourselves on the back and say there’s a lot of garbage out there. But also, it has never been a better time to be an independent creator. If we both wanted to go into media today, we would be grabbing water for hosts who get paid $60 million a year. Instead, we actually get to put our own ideas out there.
  > 
  > Tameez: Tell me about your workflow, from video ideation to posting.
  > 
  > Sinha: We don’t do short-form clips [from longer videos]. We script our shorts. That takes a ton of time. [But] long-form is the priority. It takes much more time, but you really get to have a relationship with the audience.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: When it comes to shorts, either Shreyas or I will have something that really interests us for the week. We start scripting it, then we switch the scripts and each of us does a pass on the other’s. Then we revise them and film them. That takes roughly two out of five workdays. We spend the rest of the time on long-form.
  > 
  > We’re also doing a weekly livestream to discuss the headlines with the audience and build that audience connection. So our output is two native short-form, one long-form, and one live stream per week.
  > 
  > Tameez: What are the things that make a story for A Show About the News?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: It has to interest us personally. It really shows, in a video, if we’re doing something just for the views. Our audience is super-interested in tech, AI, and Wall Street stories, so that’s [another] major thing we consider.
  > 
  > Sinha: Do we actually have something new to say about it? There’s no point in making a video that someone else has already made.
  > 
  > A lot of people have made videos about how people aren’t having kids anymore. That doesn’t mean we wouldn’t [cover that topic], but we need to have something new to say. In our video, we talked about how governments have spent tens of billions of dollars but it hasn’t resulted in any more babies. We talked about demographic trends and got into studies. We brought in a bunch of 19- and 20-year-olds and asked them why they’re not having more sex. Then we went to a bunch of older people and were like, “So, you guys were doing it hella in the 90s. What’s the secret? Let’s give it to these kids.”
  > 
  > The combination of all those things is what makes it A Show About the News video.
  > 
  > Tameez: Why is it important for you guys to be funny?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: It helps us talk about darker topics in a lighter way. Comedy also helps you build more interaction with your audience. It’s the best feeling when [people in the comments] are making jokes.
  > 
  > Sinha: We try to be funny, maybe to a deficit. In one of our worst-performing videos, we had a guy come in on an inflatable horse. Gaurav was wearing a Will Smith mask and slapped me. But sometimes it really works. We filmed this thing where we were having a shootout with Abraham Lincoln as he smoked a vape. That did great for us.
  > 
  > I listen to a lot of podcasts of serious, smart people. The way they talk, I’m like, how are you not ripping your hair off right now? How are you not just slamming your head against your desk?
  > 
  > Our videos are what we wish we could consume more of.
  > 
  > Tameez: How do you gather information? Do you do any original reporting?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: We have all these sources that we regularly read. For a lot of politics and inside scoops on Capitol Hill, Politico is great. For more niche stories, ProPublica is great. Each source has its own strength. We send stuff to each other all the time.
  > 
  > Once we’re in the research phase of a video, we do everything possible to learn about what the scope of the video is and what sources and articles can fall into that.
  > 
  > We’d be dishonest if we didn’t mention AI. We don’t use AI at all in our scripting or production process, but Perplexity has been great for finding articles and different things on specific subjects. It’s been a huge timesaver, as well, [since] the internet is dominated by paid articles rising to the top in Google.
  > 
  > Tameez: Tell me about the staging and production. Since you guys often film outside, do you have any favorite spots? Have you ever been kicked out of a location?
  > 
  > Sinha: The staging is Gaurav and me carrying around our equipment.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: We don’t carry around anything too heavy. We use a Sony FX30 on a tripod.
  > 
  > Sinha: We’ve only been kicked out of one place ever, which was 1 Worldwide Plaza on 8th Ave. [But] 99% of people are super nice and accommodating and come up and say hi. The reason we’re outside is not only so we can have the hustle and bustle [as the background], but because for the longest time we didn’t have anywhere else to film.
  > 
  > Tameez: What is your fact-checking process like? Does Morning Brew lend you any fact-checkers? How do you issue a correction if you need to?
  > 
  > Sinha: We do a lot of our own fact-checking. Corrections often occur in the way of Gaurav coming to me and being like, “Shreyas, you moron, you said this and it’s actually this.” Then we record a voiceover, or we change it in the captions, or we just put it up as text.
  > 
  > We [request comment from] every company or person we talk about, so if they have something to say, we can try to incorporate it. We do [the fact-checking] ourselves.
  > 
  > Tameez: Who is your audience? What is your relationship with them like?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: The audience is very much like who we are. They tend to be a ton of college-age or young professionals working in major cities, in finance, consulting, law, or related fields. If they’re in college, they tend to be debate kids or Model UN kids.
  > 
  > Tameez: You guys recently started doing weekly livestreams on the street. How’s that going?
  > 
  > Sinha: If you go on YouTube right now, high production quality is the expectation. It’s not like when I was young and my favorite YouTubers were clearly filming in their bedrooms. There’s so much money that is being invested into it, which is awesome, but if you want to have authentic relationships with your audiences, you need to do more.
  > 
  > [The livestreams] have been so much fun. We talk to people, ask them where they’re from, tell a story, and they have questions. It’s taking our videos to another level. We still have to learn how to grow, bring people to it, and keep them entertained.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: Earlier this year, TBPN [Technology Business Programming Network] was sold to OpenAI for $100 million. Their whole thing was live-streaming. They didn’t get that many viewers concurrently on their livestreams — everything was clips, targeting a very specific niche — but we saw how they grew through clips.
  > 
  > We also wanted to tackle news topics we don’t have the time to cover in shorts and long-form.
  > 
  > Tameez: I’m curious about how you guys sustain yourselves financially.
  > 
  > Sinha: When we started making these videos, I was writing articles on the side and freelancing so that I could pay rent. Eventually, this was able to pay rent.
  > 
  > By no means are we rich. But we really believe in the business model of digital media. It’s important to incentivize people to invest in media that is fact-checked, that is telling stories that are new and not just polarizing or viral for virality’s sake. You can’t ask people to only think of [media] as a charity expenditure or as a nonprofit, because those are the first things that go away when the market goes down, and the first thing people are going to stop cutting checks to if they don’t see the ROI.
  > 
  > We spend most of our time thinking about content, but it’s important to be creative about our business models. We always tell ourselves not to think about ourselves as employees or YouTubers, but to keep reminding ourselves that we’re founders of a startup that is trying to do something for the world.
  > 
  > The prevailing narrative that news media is doing poorly is because news media for generations was run by journalists, and that was fine because they could get cable carrier fees. But the reason they were able to do that was because they had a monopoly. You could only watch CNN, CBS, and ABC News because that’s all you had.
  > 
  > We blame consumers all the time, saying they should subscribe to newspapers, they should support TV news. We’re losing something really important by these companies being close to bankruptcy. But it’s not the consumers’ fault. There was no innovation from those companies, no leaning into newsletters. Once Morning Brew proved that they could do it, they got into podcasts, and they’re mostly all right. The New York Times is an excellent shop. But I’m yet to see that from ABC and CNN and NBC and MSNBC.
  > 
  > There’s been a huge stepping back from actually doing the job of being an inventor of new business models. That’s why we’re in this crisis. We blame consumers, we blame social media, but we really should be blaming media news executives a lot more. There’s a lot of money to be made in this. I want people to know there are people out there trying to build successful business models, not just relying on the charity of investors, rich people, and consumers.
  > 
  > Tameez: What is your business model?
  > 
  > Sinha: We sell ads, but we also bring in a new audience for Morning Brew that cross-pollinates across other products. When you watch a short-form video on one of Morning Brew’s franchises, you might be more likely to buy ad space in one of their B2B newsletters, or in their podcast, or something else. That’s a really potent business model that allows the content to be not just the product, but also to be part of this larger engine where you can monetize in a bunch of very unique and creative ways.
  > 
  > [I asked Sinha and Srivatchan to answer a few final questions via email. Their answers are below.]
  > 
  > Tameez: What are your lifestyles like? Do you have families or dependents? Homeowners or renters?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: We live in New York City, we rent our apartments. No families or dependents. We have girlfriends who you could say are very, very early investors in us.
  > 
  > Tameez: What are some of the unexpected costs of being news creators?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: Once you know how the sausage is made, you have the lens on for everything you consume. Being so deep in the process of making news videos means when we watch other creators’ content, we’re constantly analyzing their production process and creative choices. We find ourselves rewinding because we zoned out the actual content.
  > 
  > Tameez: How has your relationship to social media and information consumption changed since you started making videos?
  > 
  > Srivatchan: One under-discussed crisis with social media is “audience capture.” Basically, a creator is limited to the topics and perspectives that they know do extremely well with their viewers. It’s a tempting force to gravitate into, the constant urge to just give your audience what you know they want and very little else.
  > 
  > Tameez: What lessons do you think legacy journalism can learn from news creators and vice versa?
  > 
  > Sinha: It’s hard to say what’s legacy and what’s new these days. Morning Brew has never had a TV broadcast or print media arm, but it’s owned by a media conglomerate, Axel Springer. Other news companies, from CNN to The Wall Street Journal, have pretty successful social media presences and journalists who’ve made a name for themselves.
  > 
  > The crisis for news companies that dominated in the 20th century is that they relied on fixed distribution: cable, broadband, local stations, and papers. A random Joe couldn’t get their ideas placed in those, so the competition for viewership was highly limited.
  > 
  > Today, competition is high. Legacy journalists who struggle often blame the changing tides of information consumption, when in reality they’re just struggling to compete for attention. Many have been successful in making the transition, so we don’t think it’s fair to paint all of legacy journalism as having something to learn from news creators. But our message overall would be that traditional news shouldn’t blame consumers for not maintaining their expensive newspaper and cable subscriptions, but instead [should] figure out how to compete and win in this new age.
  > 
  > In the same vein, news creators shouldn’t pat themselves on the back that they are “new media.” Most news creators — ourselves included — often package traditional news media’s breaking stories into our own voice, but we aren’t actually at the source. As news creators who have learned to compete in this new information era, it’s our job to figure out a business model that allows for long-term investigations that break real news to be profitable.
  > 
  > Srivatchan: For journalists who have been successful in their careers but want to transition to social media, we think they severely underestimate how well-prepared they are to make the jump. They’ve already been writing strong articles and hooks to get people to click. We think journalists underestimate how that skill can translate on a platform like YouTube, where everything is dependent on the packaging that gets someone to click on your video.
  > 
  > Often, when someone from traditional media starts a new Instagram or YouTube page, they make very lo-fi content, talking to their phone with their headphones mic. I think they see influencers doing that and think viewers want authenticity. That works for some people, but our view is that investing in strong production is the baseline expectation on social media.
  > 
  > Cleo Abram goes to Antarctica for her videos with an expensive camera crew team and equipment. That’s what you have to compete with, but also with everything else. I think journalists who are successful at making the transition are often those who forget their preconceived notions of what it means to be a social media creator and instead try to create the highest-quality product they possibly can. Max Fisher is an excellent example, as a former New York Times investigative journalist who grew his YouTube channel from 0 to 500,000 subscribers within months with simple, breathable, but heavily researched storytelling.
  > 
  > Instead of seizing the social media opportunity, often, legacy journalism is falling into the trap of liquidating its reputation for more attention and to better monetize its declining assets. It’s partnering with gambling apps — CNN’s partnership with Kalshi, or [Dow Jones’] with Polymarket — and inviting inflammatory internet voices rather than the trusted ones that educate.
  > 
  > The mainstream media should use its resources to enter the edutainment space rather than the virality/inflammatory space. That doesn’t mean avoid politics — it means tackle the underlying trends and principles of politics. Audiences don’t want to hear from viral Twitter voices on CNN panels. In reality, people want to learn.

### 17. When Slate went back to its 1996 homepage, it learned some web design lessons for 2026
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/when-slate-went-back-to-its-1996-homepage-it-learned-some-web-design-lessons-for-2026/
- 发布时间：Tue, 13 Oct 2026 19:29:06 +0000
- 全文：
  > The fonts are Times New Roman and Arial Narrow, the colors are maroon and mustard, and the cursor is the 8-bit pixel hand. When Slate revived its original 1996 homepage to celebrate its 30th birthday this fall1, the team found a lot to like besides nostalgia.
  > 
  > “I’ve read Slate since 1997, when I finally had access to my own computer at my first job,” Hillary Frey, Slate’s editor-in-chief, told me recently. At the age of 51, she said, “I’m speaking as an old. But I am a direct traffic girly and I know I’m not alone.”
  > 
  > Slate’s homepage has long functioned as a “fossil of what the internet was during that time,” said Ivylise Simones, Slate’s creative director. In 1996, Slate was trying to figure out what a magazine on the internet should even look like; in 2026, it’s among the last general-interest digital-native news sites, and print magazines are dying out. “Salon is one of our only lasting contemporaries,” Frey noted. “There are other sort of fellows from that era, like Feed and Nerve. They’ve been gone for awhile.”
  > 
  > In its 30th anniversary campaign, Slate has found its fossil-like nature to be something of a selling point. (It can also be a challenge.) Over the years, the site has published a number of posts about its history and editorial and design choices. A decade ago, then–news editor Chad Lorenz wrote “A history of Slate in nine home pages.” (At launch, Slate articles included page numbers and printing instructions.) A 1998 paywall, Lorenz wrote, was “fortunately” abandoned by 1999. But the paywall returned in 2020. (It was metered at the start, but today it’s dynamic, changing based on user behavior.) For its 30th birthday, the site is running a promotion: a year of access for $19.96.
  > 
  > “Going back to our roots, there are certain elements we are starting to really love,” Simones said, and she’s thinking about how to include them in the site’s ongoing redesign. She likes the spareness and simplicity of earlier versions of the site, she said, pointing to Reddit and Discord as places that are “more reminiscent of old homepages than the current, very stylized, very gridded layout” found across the internet. A less dense homepage on desktop, she said, is “refreshing.”
  > 
  > “Our editorial guiding light is the mix,” Frey added. Slate has verticals, but they’re not emphasized in the story selection at the top of the homepage; instead, life, politics, and advice stories are mixed together and weighted similarly. “Having an image for every story you share on the homepage is just not necessary,” Simones noted.
  > 
  > Some 1990s-era headline styles still work, too, Frey has found. One of her favorites is a 2002 piece by Slate founding editor Michael Kinsley: “The secret vice of power women.”
  > 
  > “He was writing it about his wife, who found tremendous satisfaction and relaxation in watching police procedurals,” Frey said. “That’s something I still identify with, though most of them now are from Finland and Norway. 100%, this headline would play on the Slate homepage right this second.”
  > 
  > Art courtesy of Slate
  > 
  > A wrinkle for 2026: The 1996-inspired homepage had to work on mobile, even though nobody was reading Slate on a phone 30 years ago.

### 18. From bad to worse: 2026 is shaping up to be “a super El Niño year for local news”
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/from-bad-to-worse-2026-is-shaping-up-to-be-a-super-el-nino-year-for-local-news/
- 发布时间：Tue, 13 Oct 2026 19:19:01 +0000
- 全文：
  > If you thought last year was bleak, Medill’s annual State of Local News Report is here to ring the alarm bell louder.
  > 
  > What was a “creeping erosion” has now “mushroomed into a full-blown super El Niño-like event for local news, leaving widespread and long-lasting displacement in its wake,” writes Tim Franklin, founding director of the Medill Local News Initiative, in his introduction to this year’s report, released Tuesday. The Northwestern University team’s research “paints a portrait of a local news ecosystem in a state of precarity.”
  > 
  > In the past year, Medill tracked 26 new news deserts, “the largest single-year rise by far since Medill began keeping records five years ago,” Franklin writes. The previous year, only six new news deserts emerged.
  > 
  > Medill’s team says to understand that acceleration, you have to look at where newspapers are disappearing fastest now. While about 70% of the papers that disappeared over the past 20 years were based in urban or suburban communities, in recent years a greater share of newspaper closures have happened in smaller, rural and poorer communities, according to the report.
  > 
  > “When the news disappears in these counties, it stays gone,” the report states.
  > 
  > Many of the new news deserts emerged because of small rural papers shuttering in the Midwest. In fact, the report notes that Illinois lost more newspapers than any other state: 30 newspapers shuttered in the state between July 2025 and August 2026, “all small to medium-size outlets.” That means nearly one in five of the 170 newspapers that shuttered in that year was based in Illinois. The state’s losses are especially notable because it enacted one of the first public policies to preserve and grow local journalism jobs in 2024.
  > 
  > “Independent owners of small to midsize newspapers especially were already operating on a knife’s edge,” Brier Dudley writes in his examination of newspaper losses in Illinois. “This past year, their slim margins were squeezed by shrinking revenues and steeply escalating prices for newsprint, fuel and other materials.”
  > 
  > The price of newsprint rose nearly 18% over the past year, Dudley reported, as U.S. mills closed or stopped production of newsprint. That’s about five times the national rate of inflation, he noted. As of April, the United States has just one newsprint producer left.
  > 
  > A few other highlights from this year’s report:
  > 
  > The extreme urban-rural divide: “Virtually all the new news sources are in urban areas, while rural America is increasingly arid news and information territory,” Franklin writes. “And virtually all the philanthropic support for local news is in metro regions.” The report notes that there are fewer than 50 counties in the U.S. “that lack a newspaper but feature an alternative digital or broadcast outlet.” Of the 287 news outlets to start up in the last five years, nearly 80% are in urban areas.
  > 
  > Traffic declines: Digital readership is in “precipitous decline” for many outlets. According to Comscore data from more than 700 local news sites, monthly traffic has dropped by an average of nearly 2 million pageviews, or almost 30%. Meanwhile, traffic to 100 of the largest local newspapers has declined by almost 50% since 2022.
  > 
  > Local TV news: This year, for the first time, Medill’s report included local TV newsrooms. The team identified 678 local commercial TV newsrooms across the country. The overwhelming majority are located in metro areas with existing local news outlets. (“Adding commercial television to the LNI database made little impact on which counties we identify as news deserts,” the team notes.) TV news is already highly consolidated — the five largest owners control more than half of newsrooms, and the 10 largest control 75% — and further consolidation is underway, if Nexstar’s $6.2 billion acquisition of Tegna survives an antitrust lawsuit. The deal would merge the second- and fourth-largest owners of local commercial TV newsrooms.
  > 
  > Read more in the full report here.

### 19. “We’re in their shit. They’re not in ours”: USA Today Co. execs defend Palantir deal
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/were-in-their-shit-theyre-not-in-ours-usa-today-co-execs-defend-palantir-deal/
- 发布时间：Thu, 08 Oct 2026 21:39:28 +0000
- 全文：
  > USA Today Co. executives are revealing new details about a controversial deal with Palantir. In a joint interview with Fast Company’s Alex Pasternack, three executives from the country’s largest newspaper chain explained why they moved ahead with the Palantir deal despite major opposition from their own employees.
  > 
  > Mike Reed, the chairman and CEO of USA Today Co., previously said that the deal — first reported by Nieman Lab’s Hanaa’ Tameez — is part of a larger shift the company is making to become “an intelligence organization.” At a Palantir conference last month, Reed said that the company’s services were a way for USA Today Co. capitalize on “the shift to online and the data we have on consumers,” Pasternack reported.
  > 
  > In this new interview, USA Today Media president Kristin Roberts detailed some of those services for the first time:
  > 
  > In all of our entertainment stories and our sports stories, we do affiliate links to our partners. So if you’re reading a sports story, you want to buy the jersey for that team, our reporters and editors used to have to go to a file, find a relevant link, copy that link, put it in. All of that is now automated, and that’s a Palantir project.
  > 
  > Lark-Marie Anton, then a communications executive at USA Today Co., chimed in, saying that Palantir’s current scope of work is focused on audience and advertising, but in the future that could extend to social media strategy and reporting tools.
  > 
  > What stood out about Palantir was its ability to create what it calls an ‘ontology’ layer — essentially a unified model that allows different datasets and systems to work together and be acted upon in real time….The challenge isn’t collecting more data, but making better use of the data we already have.
  > 
  > In August, more than 800 USA Today Co. employees from 31 unionized newsrooms called on the company to dissolve its partnership with Palantir. In a statement, staffers named ethical issues and uncertainty over how the software would be used. Throughout the Fast Company interview, Reed and Roberts heavily criticized the NewsGuild and internal efforts to oppose the deal.
  > 
  > Roberts leveled the strongest criticisms of USA Today Co.’s unions across the country, going as far as to say the NewsGuild was lying to its members:
  > 
  > Guild leadership is saying things that are factually untrue. They prey on fear. No matter that I will appear in front of any newsroom for a standup in which they ask me anything for an hour and a half at a time, and I explain to them all of the things.
  > 
  > She continued:
  > 
  > I’ll give you an example that’s incredibly real for us. Palantir doesn’t work inside of any USA Today Company system. They don’t have access to any of the notes our reporters take. They’re not in the Microsoft suite of tools. They don’t have information directly about our consumers or our staff….They don’t work in our systems. We feed them highly controlled data. We’re in their shit. They’re not in ours.
  > 
  > Reed doubled down on these accusations, extending them to the work the NewsGuild has done organizing around AI in the company, including its push for clauses that would protect journalists from layoffs due to AI adoption.
  > 
  > “I think we’re having more success in newsrooms that are not unionized, with this new world, than those that are unionized,” Reed said.
  > 
  > Last month, USA Today Co. made significant layoffs across the company, including at its local newspapers, and reorganized its audience team. Reed, in a memo to staff, referenced AI-related traffic declines as a reason for the layoffs.
  > 
  > “USA Today Co. executives’ animosity toward their workers and unions is nothing new,” said NewsGuild president Jon Schleuss in a statement to Fast Company. “[We] fully stand behind the statement workers released when this deal with Palantir was first announced.”
  > 
  > You can read the full interview on Fast Company.

### 20. Fox News attacks the media roughly every 15 minutes, our study finds. (CNN? Every 140 minutes.)
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/fox-news-attacks-the-media-roughly-every-15-minutes-our-study-finds-cnn-every-140-minutes/
- 发布时间：Thu, 08 Oct 2026 18:38:33 +0000
- 全文：
  > In 2025, trust in journalism reached a record low. According to Gallup, only 8% of Republicans expressed trust in the news media, compared with 51% of Democrats. New data show an uptick among Republicans, but overall trust remains near this record low, and the gap between Republicans and Democrats remains substantial.
  > 
  > One of the key drivers of this lopsided decline in trust in journalism may be the extent to which news outlets spend time bashing other news outlets, or bashing the news media in general — a phenomenon we call inter-journalism disparagement. Our research team at Duke University’s DeWitt Wallace Center for Media & Democracy recently completed a study of the prominence of this phenomenon on each of the “big three” cable news networks (CNN, Fox News, MSNBC/MS NOW); whether it has increased or decreased over time; and whether there are significant differences in the rate of disparagement across networks.
  > 
  > The results were stark. Fox News far exceeds the other networks, with inter-journalism disparagement taking place once every 14.5 minutes. In contrast, CNN engages in inter-journalism disparagement roughly every 140 minutes, and MSNBC every 61 minutes.
  > 
  > To conduct this analysis, we gathered 15 years of cable news transcripts (2011-2025) for each network. We gathered a random sample of one week’s worth of programming for each year.1 With this approach, we ended up with 3,543 transcripts and almost 19 million words across the three networks, which equates to roughly 62,000 pages of text.
  > 
  > Given the volume of text to be analyzed, we trained OpenAI’s GPT-5.5 to identify attacks against the news media. We developed a 20-page instruction document to help the model code instances of disparagement. GPT-5.5 consistently achieved 99.5% accuracy at identifying inter-journalism disparagement and 100% accuracy for avoiding false negatives, compared to human-coded samples. (You can read more details about the entire training and analytical process here.)
  > 
  > We defined inter-journalism disparagement as negative statements about the conduct or content of an explicitly identified journalism target (such as a specific news organization, or the news media in general). Our definition, which is described in greater depth in the full paper, needed to be quite detailed in order for ChatGPT to produce accurate results. Examples of inter-journalism disparagement include:
  > 
  > CNN’s Dan Pfeiffer describing “political journalism writ large” as “bad,” “problematic” and “failing massively.”
  > 
  > MSNBC’s Rachel Maddow describing The Washington Post’s editorial page as “rabid” and “foaming,” and “super right-wing,” noting: “I can’t believe this is in this real newspaper.”
  > 
  > Fox News’ Mark Levin telling viewers that “MSNBC and CNN…have been lying and lying and lying to you for years.”
  > 
  > What we found when comparing the three networks tells us a lot about the dynamics of contemporary cable news and partisan journalism.
  > 
  > First, Figure 1 shows the rate of journalism disparagement across each of the big three networks. Because the volume of text that we analyzed varies across each network, our primary metric was the rate of disparagement per 1,000 words of transcript text. In this figure, we show not only the rate per 1,000 words, but we also convert that rate into a time-based frequency, using the industry standard of 1,000 words equaling approximately six minutes of air time.
  > 
  > In Figure 2, we graph the frequency of inter-journalism disparagement over time. Here, we see that the massive difference between Fox News and CNN/MSNBC persists over the 15-year time period. Still, we do see some dips in the volume of disparagement on Fox News in the two-year periods leading up to the 2016 and 2024 elections, with journalism disparagement levels on Fox News rebounding to near-peak levels in 2025. We haven’t figured out yet what to make of this pattern.
  > 
  > CNN and MSNBC seem to converge in their disparagement activity starting around 2017, with MSNBC’s disparagement rate decreasing somewhat, and CNN’s disparagement rate increasing somewhat. This pattern aligns with recent research that has shown, in other contexts, that CNN and MSNBC programming are converging in ways that suggest diminished differences between the more “centrist” CNN and the more left-leaning MSNBC.
  > 
  > A key takeaway here is that we should not think about Fox News and MSNBC as flip sides of the same partisan journalism coin. At least in relation to inter-journalism disparagement, these left- and right-leaning cable networks behave very differently. Disparaging journalism is a far more central part of Fox News programming than it is of either MSNBC or CNN programming.
  >
