  > In order to get a deeper understanding of these differences, we also identified the targets of the disparagement. These results are presented in Figures 3 through 5.
  > 
  > Figure 3 presents a breakdown of the most common targets of disparagement on Fox News. By far the most frequent target of disparagement on Fox News is “the media.” Following in a distant second is CNN, with the “mainstream media” rounding out the top three targets. Other relatively frequent targets include The New York Times, MSNBC, and The Washington Post.
  > 
  > As should be clear, Fox News devotes far more attention to disparaging journalism as an institution than it does to attacking individual news sources. This stands in stark contrast to what we see when we look at the results for CNN and MSNBC. Looking first at CNN, Figure 4 shows that the network’s most frequent target is Fox News (though with a frequency that wouldn’t crack the top three of the Fox News list).
  > 
  > Similarly, as Figure 5 shows, Fox News is also the number one target on MSNBC. Both CNN and MSNBC have the same top three, with Fox News followed by “the media” and the “right-wing media.” These instances of institutional disparagement happen far less frequently on CNN or MSNBC than they do on Fox News.
  > 
  > Finally, we were also able to identify which hosts or anchors were the most frequent disparagers of their fellow journalists. Those results can be seen in Figure 6. Given the lopsided amount of inter-journalism disparagement on Fox News, it is not surprising that this graph is dominated by Fox News personalities, with Sean Hannity taking the top spot, followed by Greg Gutfeld and Tucker Carlson (note: our graph for Howard Kurtz reflects his move from CNN to Fox in 2013).
  > 
  > This study didn’t distinguish further in terms of the nature of the disparagement across the three networks (for example, in terms of severity or legitimacy). This is something we are exploring in the next stage of this work. Nor did we examine whether viewer exposure to inter-journalism disparagement reduces trust in specific outlets or the institution of journalism. That certainly seems like a reasonable assumption, given the dramatically lower levels of trust in journalism among Republicans that we noted at the outset. We plan to investigate this relationship directly in future research.
  > 
  > But what we do see in this research is that disparagement of the institution of journalism, and of particular news outlets, is a much more prominent component of Fox News programming than it is of either CNN or MSNBC. Some researchers have proposed that this kind of high-volume disparagement of news sources that don’t tow the same partisan line takes us beyond traditional notions of partisan journalism and into the realm of “anti-media media.” The goal of anti-media media is to “weaponize the facade of journalism against the roles assigned to journalism” in a democracy. Based on our findings, Fox News fits this characterization.
  > 
  > Philip M. Napoli is the James R. Shepley Professor of Public Policy, director of the DeWitt Wallace Center for Media & Democracy, and director of undergraduate studies for the journalism and media minor in the Sanford School of Public Policy at Duke University. Adam Meskouri is a politics researcher at Harvard and Duke. Anjini Mani is an undergraduate at Duke. Grant Socol is a graduate research assistant at Duke. Harrison Walley is an undergraduate at Duke.
  > 
  > Rather than analyze the entirety of the networks’ output over this time, we created a “constructed week” for each year, which is a common practice in this kind of content analysis. This involves selecting a random Monday, Tuesday, Wednesday, etc. within each year, so that the sample of programming was reasonably representative of the year as a whole. We included all programming across all dayparts in our constructed weeks. Note: MSNBC was only available through 2023; the network became MS NOW in late 2025.

### 21. Sinclair’s new Substack repackages its local TV reporting into a video podcast
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/sinclairs-new-substack-repackages-its-local-tv-reporting-into-a-video-podcast/
- 发布时间：Wed, 07 Oct 2026 20:54:40 +0000
- 全文：
  > “How young is too young for a life in prison?”
  > 
  > “SpaceX debris is washing ashore. Who’s responsible?”
  > 
  > “Why schools get paid even when students fail.”
  > 
  > Those are the top headlines so far on Sinclair Broadcasting Group’s new Substack publication, The National Press, launched this week.
  > 
  > The National Press repackages some of the top stories from Sinclair’s 178 local news stations — which employ around 1,200 journalists — into a daily, 30-minute broadcast-style show. The program is hosted by former Newsmax and Christian Cable Network anchor Lyndsay Keith, who summarizes the stories and interviews the local journalists who first reported them. In the videos, Keith sits at a desk with a microphone; the dimly lit background is illuminated just enough to make out two American flags in a mug. (It gives off a true-crime YouTube podcast vibe.)
  > 
  > As Axios’s Sara Fischer noted on Tuesday, Sinclair has long been accused of conservative bias in its local news operation. The National Press is launching in partnership with New Media, the company behind Bari Weiss’s The Free Press, and the two publications look somewhat alike (in part because they both run on Substack). New Media staffers will help create the content and maintain the site and the company will take a portion of Sinclair’s revenue from Substack, Fischer reported.
  > 
  > The National Press is currently free to access, but has paid tiers that include ad-free viewing, community chat access, and merch. Subscriptions start at $5 per month or $50 per year. The five posts on the site so far have between seven and 14 likes each.
  > 
  > Wednesday’s episode focuses on a story about how Michigan is reconsidering lifetime sentences for people sentenced between the ages of 18 and 20. It also tackles AI regulation debates in Congress, drawing on reporting by Capitol Hill reporter Ahtra Elnashar. The show doesn’t appear to be filmed in one take like a true TV news broadcast (Keith is first seen in a black blazer, then a brown one, before switching back to black by the end of the video).
  > 
  > Another video, “Worth Noticing: School Bus Incidents,” is just two minutes long and compiles stories of school bus crashes from around the country. From the text accompanying the video:
  > 
  > We trust school buses to get our kids from the safety of our homes to school every day. So why are we seeing so many serious injuries and incidents? Is this simply a string of unrelated tragedies? Are staffing shortages the problem here? Training gaps? Weak safety protocols?
  > 
  > We’re going to be following these stories. Have you seen incidents like this in your community? Is this something that is keeping you awake at night?
  > 
  > Because these stories are horrific; we’re talking about every parent’s nightmare.
  > 
  > The National Press isn’t Sinclair’s first effort to reach a younger, video-first audience. In 2015, it launched millennial-focused news site Circa, which shuttered four years later.

### 22. “Uber agents,” undercover AI personas, and other ways newsrooms are using AI in investigations
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/uber-agents-undercover-ai-personas-and-other-ways-newsrooms-are-using-ai-in-investigations/
- 发布时间：Wed, 07 Oct 2026 19:39:59 +0000
- 全文：
  > Late last month, about 150 journalists, researchers, and technologists gathered on the 15th floor of The New York Times Building for a forum on how AI is changing investigative journalism.
  > 
  > Just outside the event space was a hallway commemorating the paper’s more than 140 Pulitzer Prize winners, dating back to 1918. One photograph had yet to be hung: a portrait of this year’s winners of the Pulitzer Prize for Investigative Reporting. For one of their winning stories, a team of Times reporters conducted an audit of the SEC’s crypto lawsuits to show weakening enforcement under the second Trump administration. The reporting was assisted by large language models (LLMs).
  > 
  > The Times isn’t the only major newsroom using AI tools in major investigations. A record-breaking eight Pulitzer awardees this year disclosed AI use to the judges. They used LLMs to organize, summarize, analyze, and translate large document dumps.
  > 
  > “At the Times we’re not doing ‘robots tell the news,’ but a number of different [kinds of AI adoption] that can make the work we do more efficient, more effective, and able to do things that otherwise would be very, very hard to do,” said Juliana Castro Varón, the Times’ senior design editor of AI initiatives, in a keynote. Often, “the most useful part [of AI] is the fact that it understands images and understands text, more than creating [them].”
  > 
  > The Times’ AI Initiatives team, a group of nine journalists and technologists leading AI strategy and deployment in the newsroom, co-hosted the event with Hacks/Hackers, a nonprofit that brings journalists and technologists together to discuss media innovation. (Hacks/Hackers hosted a summit on AI and journalism in Baltimore for the past two years.)
  > 
  > The day was packed with workshops, panels, and presentations about the promise of AI for investigative journalism, led by the technology’s most vocal advocates in newsrooms. But the limitations of these tools — including persistent hallucination problems in LLMs, concerns about vibe-coding errors, and budget constraints as AI token costs pile up — were also a common theme.
  > 
  > “AI is very good at generating leads, but much less capable of establishing publishable evidence,” said Afrooz Mosallaei, a research associate at the Center for News, Technology & Innovation, during a talk about the nonprofit’s recent report on the topic. “A lot of tasks in an investigation — including verification, source development, textual understanding, and editorial judgment — remain fundamentally human responsibilities.”
  > 
  > From search tool to “uber agent”
  > The release of the Epstein Files was a huge moment for AI in investigative journalism. Journalists across the U.S. woke up on January 30, 2026 to a Department of Justice drop of over 3.5 million pages of documents and tens of thousands of images and videos.
  > 
  > Duy Nguyen, who leads AI science research at the Times, noted that the PDFs in the Epstein Files would stack as high as the Empire State Building if they were printed out, and would take 11 years for one reporter to read. (Epstein sent an average of 65 emails every day for a decade.) It would take a reporter a month to listen to and watch all the audio and video clips.
  > 
  > Many newsrooms decided that the early organization and research into these documents was a task primed for AI technologies. Engineers from the Associated Press, NPR, and the Times led a session on how they built tools to help reporters sift through the documents.
  > 
  > NPR built an internal search tool that let reporters across the newsroom find the documents relevant to their beats, said Kriti Singh, a design technologist at NPR’s AI Labs. Her team classified documents by type (email, legal filing, flight log, witness list), grouped them by location (city, county, hotel, university), and extracted entities (making sure, for instance, that a search for “Prince Andrew” also surfaced files that mentioned “the Duke of York”).
  > 
  > The Times AI Initiatives team built the Epstein Files Engine, an internal chatbot to help reporters comb through the files. “There are so many sorts of threads that you can pull from such a big corpus, and the chat interface really allows [reporters] to dive deep,” said Nguyen. The engine’s interface was built with LibreChat, a free, open-source, self-hosted AI chat platform.
  > 
  > A few real prompts from reporters:
  > 
  > “Look at our Epstein coverage over the past three months and cross-reference with names of people most mentioned in the documents. Give me a subset of names we haven’t covered as often.”
  > 
  > “Find me Kathryn Ruemmler’s email where she said she is missing her ring in Epstein’s apartment.”
  > 
  > “Construct a timeline of Epstein’s death, give me a table that I can copy into a spreadsheet. Be very detailed. Surface witnesses, links and quotes where available.”
  > 
  > Ultimately, more than 100 Times journalists posed more than 5,000 questions to the engine, and it contributed to at least 20 published stories. “This was a signifier of just how powerful an agentic chat interface could be in a newsroom,” said Nguyen. (His team recently published an academic article detailing the tool’s development.)
  > 
  > Since then, the team has created other agentic search tools. Ahead of the Times’ 175th anniversary series, they built “Morgue Bot,” which made it easier for reporters to dive into the Times’ archive.
  > 
  > Now, the team is taking chatbots to the newsroom at large. In July, they launched News Agent, which they’re calling the “front door to AI at The New York Times.” The general-purpose internal chatbot plugs into not just the Times’ archives and the Epstein Files, but other databases, internal documents like the Times’ style guide, and the web. News Agent also allows any Times reporter to build their own custom agentic search tool, tailored to a specific project.
  > 
  > “It’s an omnibus agent or an uber agent that basically acts as a drop-in replacement for chatbots like Gemini or ChatGPT,” said Nguyen, “but specifically geared to help reporters and editors within the Times newsroom.”
  > 
  > The team’s copy for the tool reads, “Ask anything.”
  > 
  > Automating the source spreadsheet
  > Any beat reporter will tell you they have a love-hate relationship with their source spreadsheet — that Google Sheet or Excel file that lists the name, title, email address, and phone number of every source they’ve spoken to over the years. Such documents are perfect for remembering the name of a researcher you spoke to five years ago without having to dig through your inbox. But they’re tedious to update.
  > 
  > “It can become a dead document that never gets checked,” said J.D. Capelouto, a technology reporter and the newly named AI lead at Semafor.
  > 
  > To try to keep his own source spreadsheet alive and up-to-date, Capelouto made a tool in Google Apps Script, a platform that lets users build small “assistants” within Google Workspace. When he emails someone for the first time, he adds an inbox tag called “New Source.” At the end of each day, the script scans his inbox for any tagged emails, then populates a Google Sheet with all the relevant information about them, including adding a category tag and generating a logline summarizing what they discussed. (There are obvious risks in handling anonymous or sensitive sourcing, but Capelouto said the tool creates minimal new security vulnerabilities since it stays within Google’s ecosystem.)
  > 
  > From there, Capelouto started building on top of the spreadsheet. A pop-up sidebar lets him ask who might be a good person to speak with for a story; the tool combs through the spreadsheet and suggests names.
  > 
  > Every morning, a script also scans the web for the news of the day on Capelouto’s beat, cross-references those headlines with the source spreadsheet, and sends him an email suggesting a topic to cover and a source to contact. Some of the suggestions aren’t useful, Capelouto admitted, but others have actually prompted him to reach out to someone he’d forgotten about.
  > 
  > “A lot of times in journalism, some of the source management work is just saying, ‘Hey, how’s it going?” he said. “This can begin a conversation, then you get a scoop from that.”
  > 
  > The Markup’s reporters decided to go undercover. They posed as users on Match-owned apps, then reported themselves from other accounts to see how easy it was to stay on the platform.
  > 
  > There was only one problem. “We came across this ethical dilemma: Am I gonna ask my staff or myself to take a photo and put it on the Tinder account?” recalled Sisi Wei, the chief impact officer at The Markup and CalMatters.
  > 
  > Even stock photos would subject real people, who had not consented to their likeness being used in the investigation, to reputational harm. Instead, The Markup turned to AI image generators. They found a company that generates visual test imagery and had developed a way to train models without relying too heavily on images of real people.
  > 
  > “They look like real human beings, but if you turn on a light switch, you can tell that AI is here,” said Wei. But they were still “real” enough to bypass the dating apps’ content moderation filters. With these new AI headshots and personas in hand, the reporters went back on the apps and created 50 different accounts.
  > 
  > Ultimately, their investigation found that even after one of these personas was reported and kicked off a Match-owned dating app, they could get back onto the platforms without changing identifying information. Earlier this year, the 18-month long investigation won a SABEW Award for best technology business reporting.
  > 
  > “AI became a way for us to protect our own staff from needing to be a part of the experiment,” said Wei.

### 23. Nieman Lab has a new look
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/nieman-lab-has-a-new-look/
- 发布时间：Wed, 07 Oct 2026 19:37:51 +0000
- 全文：
  > You may have noticed that things look a little different around here. We’ve just launched the third major redesign in Nieman Lab history. Hope you like it!
  > 
  > My No. 1 goal for this design (internal codename: Roscoe) was increasing design diversity on article pages — to enable a greater degree of art direction. In our previous design, we’ve had three basic layout choices for an article — which basically came down to how large we wanted to play its art. Now we have vastly more room to tie the design to the article — multiple layouts, multiple arrangements of headlines/decks/art, multiple background colors, multiple optical sizes, and more. Here’s a sampling:
  > 
  > The new homepage gives more prominence to our What We’re Reading section — which we update 10 to 20 times each weekday, meaning there’ll be something fresh atop the homepage frequently through the day. It’s got modules to highlight two of our most important topics — AI and local news — and a place for older stories still worth highlighting. Search and subscribe have improved UX. Our footer now aligns with the other Nieman Foundation websites. (Plus: dark mode!)
  > 
  > For about a decade, we’ve divided our stories into “hubs”: Business Models, Mobile & Apps, Audience & Social, Aggregation & Discovery, and Reporting & Production. Those had started to feel a little outmoded; it’s been a while since “mobile” was anything other than the middle of the mainstream, for instance. So we’ve replaced them with six “beats” more attuned to the times: AI & Journalism, Search & Platforms, Audience & Creators, Local News, Reporting & Production, and Business & Revenue. With a little AI assistance, we’ve populated those collections with all 12,000-plus articles we’ve published since 2008.
  > 
  > Those 12,000-plus articles were one of the biggest challenges — making sure they all made the transition cleanly. More than 100 of those articles used custom CSS; each one had to be checked (and most of them updated) to match new layout structures and typefaces. In all, there are more than 100,000 unique URLs on niemanlab.org that now have a new look. And we now have enough history to finish off the homepage with stories we’ve published on this day over the decades.
  > 
  > Our new primary typefaces are TypeTogether’s Literata as the serif and Bakken & Bæck’s Schibsted Grotesk as the sans. (Schibsted Grotesk is, of course, the custom typeface designed for the European media house.) My thanks to the Freight collection (Text, Display, and Sans) for its decade-plus of typographic service.
  > 
  > I’ve designed and coded every iteration of niemanlab.org since founding the Lab in 2008, and it’s been a point of perverse pride for me to be a journalist with some technical ability. But it also means that, over the past 18 years, our backend has accumulated the handcoded cruft of a zillion PHP bugfixes and CSS hacks. So this time I was happy to do the vast majority of the work in Claude Code, which made some of the more ambitious ideas I had far easier to implement. The code is clean and organized in a way that should make it easier to build further.

### 24. Subscribers are up, but growth is down. Here’s what worker-owned Hell Gate is trying next.
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/subscribers-are-up-but-growth-is-down-heres-what-worker-owned-hell-gate-is-trying-next/
- 发布时间：Tue, 06 Oct 2026 18:06:53 +0000
- 全文：
  > With around $1.5 million in revenue, 2026 is on track to be a break-even year for the worker-owned local news site Hell Gate.
  > 
  > “We’ve spent a lot of money growing this year,” Hell Gate co-founder Nick Pinto told me, “so the fact we’re ending the year in the same place with regards to our bank account, it’s basically good. But it would be better if we were ending with more money than we started with.”
  > 
  > The New York City-based local news org has about 11,000 paid subscriptions — and exceeded $1 million in annual recurring revenue for the first time. That subscriber support is critical, the report notes, because “philanthropy is fickle” and “advertising just doesn’t pay the bills in 2026.” (Hell Gate made more than $20,000 in advertising so far this year, primarily on newsletter ads, compared to $660,000 in subscription revenue in the first three quarters.)
  > 
  > “We have said for many years that subscriptions are also more stable than other revenue sources, because, barring a national economic crisis, subscribers are unlikely to desert us all at once. That qualifier feels a bit less hypothetical and remote these days,” the report goes on, linking to a headline about how an AI crash would cause a recession and global economic stagnation, “but our philosophy is unchanged.”
  > 
  > Hell Gate expects a revenue boost thanks to a new tax credit from New York State. To collect the $157,000, its worker-owners have to jump through a series of hoops, including refiling their personal taxes for 2025.
  > 
  > Like its fellow worker-owned outlet Defector, Hell Gate offers an unusually candid look at its own finances in its annual report. Hell Gate’s openness about its business is a gift to anyone else trying to build (or grow) a news outlet. Many in the news industry, for example, are also trying to solve one of the major problems Hell Gate lays out in its annual report: turning free readers into paying subscribers.
  > 
  > Subscriber growth at Hell Gate slowed from 69% to 22% in the last year. Nearly 64,000 people currently get at least one of Hell Gate’s three newsletters. One conversion move that worked? Randomly withholding the free Morning Spew newsletter.
  > 
  > “The fact that the free subscribers have grown faster than our paying subscribers suggests to me that we need to be more aggressive about figuring out how to work them in,” Pinto said. “Gentle encouragement and inducing guilt is definitely one powerful tool. The other one is turning off the free stuff every once in awhile in a stochastic, destabilizing way, so that they’re like, ‘Oh, I actually really did want to read that newsletter today.”
  > 
  > Here’s the top of one newsletter that successfully nudged some “Hell-Gate-curious freeloaders” to become paying subscribers:
  > 
  > Hell Gate, founded by five journalists in 2022, now has 10 employees, including a couple of part-timers. Currently, only full-time editorial employees can become worker-owners, and only after a vesting period. Shares can only be owned by workers so when co-founder Esther Wang departed this summer, the rest of the owners got the shares back under a buyback policy.
  > 
  > “When we organized Hell Gate, in our fevered idealism, we wrote it into our operating agreement that there’s no way for anybody to make any money selling Hell Gate,” Pinto said.
  > 
  > “To the extent that we contemplated the possibility that we’d even still exist [four years later], I don’t think any of us thought we’d ever want to leave,” Pinto added. “The arrangement makes ownership stakes about control of the direction of the company rather than about, you know, accruing value on an investment. That means our ownership is a thing that keeps us pulling together rather than having us fight over money.”
  > 
  > But, even in worker-owned news organizations, money can be a source of tension. “Compensation and how to structure it has reliably been the single most difficult conversation for Hell Gate owners,” the report notes.
  > 
  > Hell Gate once paid journalists the same salary. Last year, it paid reporters $75,000/year and its two editors $85,000/year. Now, in 2026, Hell Gate salaries range between $83,000 and $100,000, “according to a slightly complicated formula intended to reflect both longevity with the company and the extra work and responsibility carried by editors.” The news org expects to keep tinkering with the pay structure.
  > 
  > “The wise words of one of Hell Gate’s advisory board members continue to be borne out: In setting compensation, the best you can hope for is to minimize the inevitable dissatisfaction,” the report reads.
  > 
  > You can read the full report here.

### 25. A study of nonprofit newsrooms reveals some cracks in the model’s foundation
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/a-study-of-nonprofit-newsrooms-reveals-some-cracks-in-the-models-foundation/
- 发布时间：Tue, 06 Oct 2026 18:00:07 +0000
- 全文：
  > With an average of 10 local newspapers closing each month, joining the thousands that have met that fate already, conditions out there are grim. Largely in response, more than 6,000 nonprofit foundations have made major investments in local news initiatives, totaling some half a billion dollars or more in direct funding for journalism.
  > 
  > As a result, the number of U.S. nonprofit news organizations has been growing by double-digit percentages year-over-year.
  > 
  > It’s fair to question whether this is sustainable. But in the meantime, at least, what’s not to like about this arrangement — this major push to subsidize local journalism at a time when the for-profit model for it is rapidly vanishing?
  > 
  > Behind the scenes, it seems, there are cracks in the foundation, ones that appear to call for greater scrutiny to understand how sustainable and effective this model truly is moving forward.
  > 
  > As described in an ethnographic study published recently in Journalism & Mass Communication Quarterly, Jocelyn McKinnon-Crowley of the University of Memphis spent four months in late 2023 and early 2024 embedded in a four-person nonprofit newsroom in a midsized Rust Belt city, and also interviewed news workers at seven other nonprofit outlets. She focused on the question of labor: What is the impact of this type of journalism on the work and the workers involved?
  > 
  > Her main finding is blunt: “The emphasis on funding, on money, and on organizational concerns has become the top priority for individuals working for nonprofit news entities.” And, as journalists “busy themselves” with these administrative chores and organizational imperatives — to keep grant money coming in, to keep funders happy, etc. — it not only comes at the expense of the journalism product but also extracts a heavy toll of unpaid labor from the news workers involved.
  > 
  > The situation, McKinnon-Crowley writes, “leads to a double worker exploitation where journalists feel morally obligated to do good work as journalists and to do good work for a morally just (nonprofit) organization.”
  > 
  > This exploitation, she says, is perpetuated by the nature of nonprofit reliance, as foundations often “take decision-making power away from journalists and the institution of journalism.” A clear example of this, she says, is the grant-making process, which can require an undue level of administrative burden and can force journalists to put the priorities and interests of funders ahead of those of the community.
  > 
  > The result, she contends, is a pernicious cycle:
  > 
  > “Human versus AI news anchors: A survey study on audience satisfaction and the emotional connection.” By Yuan Ji, in Journalism. China has been one of the world’s leading laboratories for AI news anchors. The experiment began in earnest in 2018, when the Xinhua News Agency introduced a virtual news presenter capable of delivering news around the clock. The pitch was simple: AI could provide a tireless, endlessly replicable alternative to a human anchor.
  > 
  > Nearly a decade into that experiment, however, audiences don’t seem ready to trade in the humans.
  > 
  > That’s the finding that stands out from a new study of 343 Chinese news consumers by researcher Yuan Ji. The study examined how audiences perceive AI anchors, including their engagement, emotional connection, and satisfaction compared with human anchors.
  > 
  > The numbers tell a fairly clear story. Only 4.96% of respondents said they found AI anchors very engaging. Another 25.07% said they were somewhat engaging. So nearly three-quarters of respondents did not describe AI anchors as engaging in either of those ways.
  > 
  > The satisfaction gap is even more pronounced. Just 5.25% said they were more satisfied with AI anchors than with human anchors. Another 24.78% said they were equally satisfied.
  > 
  > That means roughly seven in 10 respondents did not report being equally or more satisfied with AI anchors.
  > 
  > For news organizations, the finding raises an important question: What happens when technology solves a problem that audiences don’t necessarily think needs solving?
  > 
  > AI anchors offer obvious efficiencies. They can work continuously, be reproduced across broadcasts and locations, and potentially reduce the costs associated with producing and presenting routine news. But those advantages matter less if audiences don’t find the result engaging or satisfying.
  > 
  > That’s an important consideration for journalists as AI moves deeper into news production. The technology is getting better at mimicking the mechanics of journalism. But journalism is more than mechanics. As Yuan Ji found, audiences also respond to the people delivering the news — and to the sense of connection, credibility, and human presence those people provide.
  > 
  > The finding doesn’t mean AI won’t change journalism. It does suggest that news organizations should think beyond what AI can do and consider what audiences actually value. Efficiency may be one reason to use an AI anchor, but efficiency alone doesn’t guarantee engagement or satisfaction.
  > 
  > For now, the numbers offer some good news for human journalists in television. AI may be ready for the news desk, but audiences, at least for now, still seem to want a person sitting behind it.
  > 
  > “Government-funded watchdog journalism? Analyzing Canadian media workers’ self-perceptions of independence under the local journalism initiative.” By Chris Arsenault, in Journalism Practice. Government funding might seem like an obvious threat to journalistic independence. But Canadian journalists who receive public money say it hasn’t interfered with their work. Instead, many still point to advertisers as the bigger threat.
  > 
  > This new study explores the journalists working under Canada’s Local Journalism Initiative, a federal program that has provided more than $128 million since 2019 to support hundreds of reporting positions at news organizations across the country.
  > 
  > Researcher Chris Arsenault interviewed 22 journalists whose positions are supported by the program. What they said complicates the familiar concern in journalism, that accepting government money inevitably makes it harder for reporters to hold power to account.
  > 
  > The journalists certainly recognized the optics. Many worried that government funding could make audiences question their independence or create the appearance of a conflict. But none said the funding had actually changed what they could report. None could point to a story they had avoided or softened because of the government support. And none could identify a source who had treated them differently because their salary was publicly funded.
  > 
  > When journalists described actual threats to their independence, they were more likely to talk about advertisers.
  > 
  > Several reporters recalled situations in which advertisers had made it more difficult to scrutinize local businesses, organizations or other powerful figures. For these journalists, commercial pressure wasn’t an abstract concern about appearances — it was something they had experienced in their newsrooms.
  > 
  > That distinction is worth considering in an industry where the traditional business model has collapsed in many local communities. Journalists may understandably worry about accepting money from the government. But if the alternative is a newsroom with fewer reporters, fewer resources, and greater dependence on advertisers, public funding may sometimes give journalists more room to do independent reporting, not less.
  > 
  > The journalists weren’t convinced the funding program could fix the larger financial problems facing local news, however. Many saw it as temporary help rather than a long-term solution.
  > 
  > The findings suggest journalists may need to think differently about where threats to independence actually come from — whether from a dependence on government or advertising or some other source. Public funding may not eliminate the challenges facing local journalism, but it seems positioned to give reporters the resources and freedom to do work that commercial pressures have sometimes made harder.
  > 
  > “‘This is how journalism has always been done’: White neutrality, newsroom norms, and an AI intervention in source diversification.” By Gavin Adamson, Asmaa Malik, Shari Okeke, and Jisele Bailey-Hay, in Journalism.
  > 
  > Adding AI to the newsroom does not necessarily help journalists find better sources in the moment. In a new study of Canadian journalists, the technology was more useful as a mirror, helping reporters look back at their coverage and see whose voices they routinely include — and whose they don’t.
  > 
  > Researchers interviewed 12 journalists at a mid-sized Canadian daily and demonstrated an AI tool designed to track the types of sources appearing in news stories. The tool categorizes sources into groups such as politicians, authorities, experts, organizations, and people speaking from personal experience.
  > 
  > The journalists generally supported more inclusive sourcing. They saw it as important to accurate, fair and accountable journalism. But they also described why it can be difficult to put that ideal into practice.
  > 
  > Time pressure, unsurprisingly, was a major obstacle. Reporters working on deadline often turn to sources they know they can reach quickly, including government officials, police, academic experts, and other institutional voices. Finding people outside those established networks takes time, particularly in newsrooms that have fewer reporters and less time for relationship-building.
  > 
  > The result can be a heavy reliance on white and official sources, with the same voices and institutions appearing repeatedly in the news even when journalists recognize that their coverage would benefit from a broader range of perspectives.
  > 
  > In this study, the AI tool was found to do little to change that during the reporting process. The journalists did not envision using it to tell them whom to call for a story or to help them find a source before deadline. Instead, they saw value in using it afterward, reviewing individual stories or larger collections of coverage to identify patterns in their sourcing.
  > 
  > That makes the technology less of a reporting assistant and more of a feedback mechanism. It could help a reporter or editor notice, for example, how often official sources appear in their coverage or how rarely people with direct experience of an issue are quoted.
  > 
  > That kind of reflection could matter if newsrooms use it as part of their regular editorial conversations. The technology can make patterns visible that are otherwise easy to overlook amid the daily rush of producing the news.
  > 
  > But seeing a problem is not the same as fixing it. An AI system can identify who appeared in a story. It cannot build relationships with communities, earn the trust of unfamiliar sources, or give reporters more time to do the work.
  > 
  > Overall, AI can help newsrooms see their sourcing habits more clearly, but it cannot, at present, create the conditions necessary to change them. If source diversity is going to become a sustained part of journalism, it will require newsroom expectations and resources to support it, not simply another piece of technology.
  > 
  > “Stuck between democracy and a layoff: Connecting journalists’ personal disillusionment to overall normative press evaluations.” By Alexis Haskell, in Journalism Practice.
  > 
  > Many journalists enter the profession believing they can make a difference. They expect to inform the public, explain complicated issues, give people a voice, and contribute to democracy. This new study suggests that when those expectations collide with the realities of working in journalism, journalists can become disillusioned with more than their own jobs.
  > 
  > Alexis Haskell surveyed 695 U.S. journalists and found that disillusionment is a measurable experience among American journalists. The concept is different from burnout, the researcher argues. Burnout describes exhaustion and being overwhelmed by work. Disillusionment describes a deeper break between what journalists expected their careers to be and what they actually experienced.
  > 
  > On average, the journalists surveyed were more disillusioned than not. Haskell measured the concept through their feelings about their work, their expectations for the profession and their sense of purpose.
  > 
  > That disillusionment was also reflected in how journalists viewed journalism as a whole. Those who were more disillusioned were more likely to give the press lower marks for five of six democratic roles examined by the study. They were less positive about journalism’s ability to inform, analyze, provide a forum for public discussion, foster social empathy, and mobilize audiences.
  > 
  > The strongest relationships were found in analysis and the public forum. More disillusioned journalists were particularly likely to believe the press was failing to help people understand complicated issues or provide space for public conversation.
  > 
  > There was one exception: Disillusionment was not related to journalists’ evaluations of investigative journalism.
  > 
  > Haskell suggests that investigative work may occupy a different place in journalism’s professional culture. It is highly prestigious, frequently celebrated through major awards and often associated with individual achievement. The other roles are more directly tied to journalism’s service to audiences and communities.
  > 
  > That difference gets at what makes disillusionment important. Journalists who are disillusioned aren’t necessarily saying they can no longer do good journalism. They may instead be questioning whether journalism, as a profession, is accomplishing what they believed it was supposed to accomplish.
  > 
  > The finding connects the personal experience of working in journalism with a broader assessment of the field. When journalists encounter low wages, layoffs, shrinking staffs, and other pressures, the consequences may extend beyond their own careers.
  > 
  > They may begin to lose faith not just in the job, but in journalism’s ability to fulfill the public purpose that drew them to it in the first place.
  > 
  > A previous version of this piece stated that 550 nonprofit newsrooms operate in the U.S. This is inaccurate and it is difficult to estimate the number of nonprofit newsrooms in the country.

### 26. ChatGPT is adding real cartoonists’ signatures to fake New Yorker cartoons
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/chatgpt-is-adding-real-cartoonists-signatures-to-fake-new-yorker-cartoons/
- 发布时间：Mon, 05 Oct 2026 20:01:27 +0000
- 全文：
  > At the pearly gates, Dolly Parton, in a sparkling gown, stands arm in arm with Tim Curry, dressed as Dr. Frank-N-Furter. “I knew I chose the right plus one,” Parton says to God, who is standing at heaven’s reception desk.
  > 
  > That’s the premise of a cartoon that went viral on Twitter in the days after the legendary entertainers’ deaths in late August. One tweet of the cartoon received 25,000 likes, while other versions of the image circulated on Facebook, Instagram, and Bluesky.
  > 
  > In the bottom right corner of the cartoon is the pen name “BLOPER,” the signature of New Yorker cartoonist Brendan Loper. But Loper didn’t draw the cartoon, or sign it. It was generated by ChatGPT and first posted to Facebook by a Dolly Parton fan. In the comments section of her post, she wrote she had simply asked ChatGPT to make “a New Yorker-style cartoon.”
  > 
  > “I had people writing to me about this cartoon, asking if it was mine,” Loper told me. First came a text from his twin brother, whose friend had seen the AI-generated image. Emails and DMs on social media from strangers followed. “It was very surreal,” he said.
  > 
  > These days, cartoon signatures are more than a byline. In an era where cartoons are regularly stripped from their original context and circulated on blogs and social media without proper licensing, they can also act as a watermark that ties an illustration to its original creator.
  > 
  > “The signature for me is the certificate of authenticity. I only put it on work that I’m putting on the internet that I feel needs to be recognized as from my hand,” Loper told me. “If I put my name on it, it’s official, it’s mine. It really is a brand. It’s the mark that I made.”
  > 
  > Loper isn’t the only New Yorker cartoonist whose signature has been reproduced by ChatGPT. In the same online forums are dozens of images with those of other prominent contributors, including Harry Bliss, Emily Flake, Joe Dator, Pat Byrnes, Peter Vey, and Jason Adam Katzenstein. In my own tests, ChatGPT output cartoons with signatures of some of the magazine’s most established cartoonists, past and present, including George Booth, Liza Donnelly, Ellis Rosen, and Saul Steinberg. In all, I documented more than 15 New Yorker cartoonists whose signatures have been used by OpenAI’s image generator without permission or compensation.
  > 
  > “We believe the future of creativity is one that is fundamentally human, and our focus is on building tools that empower human creativity and creators,” said a spokesperson for OpenAI in a statement. “We very much appreciate the community flagging bugs and unintended behavior by our models, so we can address them.”
  > 
  > After I notified OpenAI of the issue, ChatGPT began to output a new message when I asked it to generate New Yorker-style cartoons: “This prompt may violate our guardrails concerning similarity to third-party content.” Still, as of the publication of this story, it continues to sign some of the generic cartoons it generates with the names of real New Yorker cartoonists.
  > 
  > OpenAI declined to answer my questions about how its models might have ingested signatures from The New Yorker’s archives during the training process.
  > 
  > Even if Condé Nast did want to license cartoons to OpenAI, The New Yorker’s standard cartoonist contract leaves little room for it. The magazine’s two cartoon editors work with a roster of freelancers who submit their work on spec and only get paid if a cartoon is selected for publication. I reviewed several templatized contracts for New Yorker contributing cartoonists, none of which permit licensing for AI training purposes. While The New Yorker has the right to publish commissioned cartoons first, it is not a “work for hire” arrangement and the cartoonist retains ownership of their copyright.
  > 
  > So how did OpenAI’s models learn those signatures? It’s possible they were scraped from the internet. Court documents released last month in the ongoing New York Times v. OpenAI lawsuit reveal the extent to which OpenAI knowingly circumvented publishers’ paywalls while developing ChatGPT. Unredacted Microsoft memos described ChatGPT as a “product that destroys its supply chain” and the data scraped to train its underlying LLMs as “the largest theft of labor in human history.”
  > 
  > In another memo, Jack Clark, then the policy director of OpenAI, wrote, “[O]ur work on AI and Creativity is going to increasingly lead to us creating systems that substitute for the labor of the people that define the ‘culture’ of society.” (Clark went on to co-found Anthropic.)
  > 
  > Many of the cartoonists I spoke to for this story echoed Clark’s sentiment.
  > 
  > “It’s an uncanny simulacrum of an impression of a joke,” said Jason Adam Katzenstein, who has been publishing cartoons in The New Yorker since 2014. “But it’s not the point for it to actually be funny or good. The point is that it’s trying to deal a death blow to labor. It’s trying to render individual cartoonists obsolete because we cost money and take time.”
  > 
  > Katzenstein was part of the class action lawsuit filed against Anthropic in 2024 by members of the Authors Guild, the largest professional organization for published writers in the United States. Katzenstein’s cartoons had been used in an anthology published by Harper Collins, which was pirated and used by Anthropic to train its Claude models.
  > 
  > Last year, a judge ruled that training Claude on the authors’ work was fair use, but that Anthropic’s decision to download and maintain copies of the pirated books was a form of mass copyright infringement. In July, a federal court approved a settlement in the case, granting a $1.5 billion payout to authors and publishers — the largest in the history of U.S. copyright law.
  > 
  > Katzenstein considers the reproduction of his signature by ChatGPT to be more than just a violation of intellectual property; to him, it’s closer to false impersonation. “[ChatGPT] is attaching my name to work that I do not endorse or like. It’s slop, and unlike the other slop that I’ve encountered, this is slop that’s pretending to be me.”
  > 
  > Several cartoonists I spoke to also differentiated ChatGPT’s ability to mimic their illustration style from the reproduction of their signature.
  > 
  > “I’ve had people hack my credit card,” said Joe Dator, a New Yorker contributor for the past 20 years. “That feels like less of a violation than this. When they hacked my credit card, they didn’t dress up like me.”
  > 
  > Alternatively, Grimmelmann says New Yorker cartoonists could have grounds for a “right of publicity” case, a body of state-based law that protects individuals from the unauthorized use of their name or identity. For that, there would need to be proof that the AI-generated cartoons were used for a commercial purpose, not just as a joke or gag.
  > 
  > While I found no evidence that these AI-generated cartoons are being bought or sold, there are signs that image generators are already displacing the work of professional cartoonists.
  > 
  > Last year, The Baltimore Sun fired Kevin Kallaugher, a Pulitzer finalist who had published editorial cartoons in the paper for more than 30 years. And earlier this year, the Sun began regularly publishing AI-generated cartoons in its opinions section. A quick check with OpenAI’s watermark identification tool shows the illustrations were created with OpenAI’s products.
  > 
  > “[We] condemn the use of AI for its theft from artists, its misuse of our planet’s resources, and its threat to original, creative thought,” Marc Murphy, the president of The Association of American Editorial Cartoonists, wrote in an open letter published last month. “The Sun is not alone among American newspapers taking this degrading way out.”
  > 
  > The New Yorker hasn’t taken any such turn toward publishing AI-generated cartoons, though none of the contributors I spoke to said they could support themselves solely on their commissions from the magazine. A New Yorker cartoon is a calling card, and work for the publication opens doors to commercial work, book illustrations, and other gigs that together make up a livelihood. It is this work that is most threatened by AI, they said.
  > 
  > In 1998, Pat Byrnes published his first cartoon in The New Yorker under the pen name “P.Byrnes.” He went on to build a decades-long career with that name. Since he first began drawing cartoons as a teenager, he has always signed it with a period at the end.
  > 
  > “It was like signing a letter,” he said. “Here’s my statement — my gift to you to accept or reject.”
  > 
  > Across the more than a dozen ChatGPT cartoons I found signed with Byrnes’ name, every one ended with a period.

### 27. How a plan to turn the Spokane Spokesman-Review into “The People’s Paper” ended in a newsroom revolt
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/how-a-plan-to-turn-the-spokane-spokesman-review-into-the-peoples-paper-ended-in-a-newsroom-revolt/
- 发布时间：Mon, 05 Oct 2026 18:44:52 +0000
- 全文：
  > This report was produced in a collaboration between InvestigateWest and RANGE.
  > 
  > When the Spokesman-Review announced plans to transfer Spokane’s 143-year-old, family-owned newspaper to a nonprofit founded by executive editor Rob Curley, it took over the front page.
  > 
  > “The People’s Paper,” read the April 16, 2025, headline, saying ownership would transfer “to you, the community.”
  > 
  > Key to the plan was Curley, the folksy self-proclaimed “journalism nerd” who burst into Spokane a decade ago, declaring a love for Gonzaga University basketball rivaled only by his love for Disney. The transition to Comma Community Journalism Lab was cast in media industry publications not only as a way to save Spokane’s daily newspaper, but as part of a growing movement to resurrect dying papers by making them less dependent on ads and corporate ownership and more on philanthropy and community partnerships. As proof of concept, the paper had already added several so-called “community-funded” journalists to its staff.
  > 
  > But in the Spokesman-Review newsroom this summer, that hopeful vision ran into persistent concerns about Curley’s leadership. More than a dozen current and former employees, along with contemporaneous records reviewed by InvestigateWest and RANGE, reveal how the newsroom had raised concerns about Curley’s management for years, culminating in his suspension from the paper on Aug. 7 and a vote of no confidence from the union, the Spokane Editorial Society. He is currently on leave as the paper conducts an internal investigation.
  > 
  > Employees said stories that might have angered powerful community figures or potential donors were killed or curtailed, and that they felt he retaliated against workers he viewed as disloyal. Staffers questioned his financial oversight of Comma, which hired his own son at a salary topping most reporters’ and recruited out-of-town executives to serve as top editors before Comma had met its fundraising goal. Current and former Spokesman-Review employees also described workplace complaints involving Curley and one of those editors, including allegations of inappropriate lewd and sexual comments around employees, including teenage interns.
  > 
  > Curley declined to comment for this article. In a brief phone interview, Curley’s attorney, Patrick Kirby, who specializes in employment and wrongful termination cases, said, “It’s all inaccurate,” in reference to a list of claims InvestigateWest and RANGE provided to Curley prior to publication. Kirby hung up the call when a reporter pressed him on which claims specifically he believed were inaccurate.
  > 
  > The top of Page 1 of The Spokesman-Review, April 16, 2025.
  > 
  > Tensions in the newsroom came to a head in the first week of August, as the paper rallied to cover the most destructive wildfires in Spokane’s history and the imminent ownership transfer approached at the end of the month.
  > 
  > Some staffers were alarmed at the prospect of working for an organization Curley founded, with even fewer checks on his authority.
  > 
  > “The terror had been building through advertising, circulation, and this newsroom each day, getting closer to the day of knowing we would have to work for Rob without the filter of human resources and the publisher,” said one employee, speaking anonymously for fear of retribution.
  > 
  > Reporters had grown increasingly frustrated with Comma editors meddling with stories, and Curley tried to fire a reporter who pushed back, employees say. But instead, when other Spokesman-Review editors backed the reporter, publisher William “Stacey” Cowles suspended Curley, citing “several personnel complaints” against him.
  > 
  > It wasn’t until after Curley’s suspension that reporters were told news that called the Comma plan into question. In a meeting days after Curley had been kicked out of the building, Spokesman-Review management shared that despite public claims by Curley that several reporters were paid for by community donors — a source of optimism for the Comma plan — none of the paper’s reporting positions were currently community-funded.
  > 
  > There were “audible gasps,” according to several reporters in the room. The paper had been promoting its community-funded reporters online, removing paywalls for stories written by those journalists. One reporter remembered how at fundraising events, Curley would point to a journalist’s face on a big screen and tell donors that their money paid for journalists’ salaries. Even the reporters took pride in sharing the claim with readers.
  > 
  > “I’ve been lying on his behalf without even knowing it,” one reporter said.
  > 
  > Cowles, the publisher, said he had no comment on the claims about Curley until the conclusion of the internal investigation. He said the paper is targeting Oct. 30 to close the transaction with Comma. Bill Simer, Comma Community Journalism Lab board chair, did not comment for this article.
  > 
  > Saving local journalism
  > Before joining the Spokesman-Review in 2016, Curley had been praised in some journalism circles as a leader in transitioning struggling newspapers away from print and into the digital world. In naming Curley as the Spokesman-Review’s new top editor, Cowles said Curley had “a proven record of delivering news that matters” and that his “innovative work” at previous newspapers helped them win top journalism awards “including a Pulitzer Prize,” the announcement said.
  > 
  > But reporters at his previous gigs viewed him differently: as a mercurial leader who hyped expensive, ineffective initiatives, sacrificing journalism ethics to keep up cozy relationships with powerful community institutions and politicians. The Orange County Register in Southern California was criticized for favorable coverage of Disney under Curley, who served as the paper’s top editor between 2014 and 2016 and proudly said he wore a Mickey Mouse garment every day. He also faced backlash when he revised an article two months after it had been published at the request of a politician, despite police reports and other records that didn’t support the change. Curley changed the story over the objections of the reporter and did not note the changes for the public with a correction or editor’s note — a decision journalism ethicists called an “unethical overreach.”
  > 
  > Journalists from the Las Vegas Sun in Nevada had their own problems with him. After he left the Sun in 2012, they noticed that Curley kept getting credit for the paper’s Pulitzer Prize in 2009 — credit that Cowles repeated in the 2016 hiring announcement. Sun employees clarified to the Inlander, a Spokane alt-weekly, that Curley, in fact, had nothing to do with the series that won the Pulitzer. Curley said he’d never told anyone he won a Pulitzer, blaming a series of misunderstandings for it showing up time and time again in his biography.
  > 
  > Curley stuck around Spokane, undaunted by the controversies from his past. And with layoffs and buyouts shrinking the newsroom in a struggling industry, Curley two years ago began testing the Comma pitch among staff by signaling that it would save their jobs. Reporters said he told them privately that they’d receive big raises once the transfer happened. If Comma didn’t move forward, he said the Cowles family would likely lay off staff or maybe sell the locally owned paper to one of the hedge fund–owned companies known for downsizing newsrooms, one current employee recalled.
  > 
  > Reporters felt Curley had compromised editorial independence at the Spokesman as well.
  > 
  > Staffers took issue with Curley altering an article in 2025 about a bond proposal from Spokane Public Schools. In the article, reporters wrote a passage explaining how the proposal could raise property taxes. The story had been cleared by the typical story editors, but late the night before the story was published, Curley had a copy editor change that passage “to be more in line with what the school district wanted us to be saying,” said Emry Dinman, one of the article’s two reporters. “My editors were all as outraged as I was.”
  > 
  > Staffers knew Comma was trying to partner with the school district as part of the ownership transition, though Dinman said he didn’t want to speculate on the intentions behind the late-night edit.
  > 
  > Current and former staffers also said Curley didn’t like articles that were critical of Gonzaga, a private university downtown most known for its basketball team that Curley frequently celebrated in his columns.
  > 
  > Rebecca Hansen-White, a former reporter at the Spokesman-Review from 2018 to 2020, said that during the COVID-19 pandemic she wrote a story about Gonzaga’s plans for an isolation facility, which included some light criticism from a local politician for the private university using government money to do so. But the story was never published. When Hansen-White asked what happened, she recalls an editor told her something to the effect of “that may have been one Rob didn’t want to run,” though she was never able to confirm if Curley spiked it himself.
  > 
  > “That was very frustrating. I worked hard on this and I’m going out to the community and I’m getting in these tough conversations with sources,” she said. “And then I come back to the newsroom and find that my work has been changed or removed without an explanation.”
  > 
  > Comma eventually set up shop on the Gonzaga campus in 2024.
  > 
  > The two editors who Curley hired for Comma in fall 2025 did little to ease reporter concerns about outside interference. Donna Wares and Ron Sylvester had both worked with Curley at the Orange County Register, and when Curley brought them into the Spokesman-Review newsroom, an email sent to the staff described their roles until the transfer as “consultants” — Sylvester for “newsroom metrics and trust initiatives,” and Wares for “enterprise story assignment, editing and Northwest Passages author recruitment.” Northwest Passages is the nonprofit’s book club and event series.
  > 
  > That distinction was crucial for Spokesman-Review staffers, who considered it a breach of journalistic integrity to have non-staffers weigh in on editorial decisions, even if it came from people who would one day be their editors.
  > 
  > “We were told through the union that Comma editors would not be able to touch our stories until the transition happened,” said Emily White, a former reporter who left the Spokesman at the end of August.
  > 
  > But that’s not what happened.
  > 
  > “They were tampering with our stories with no permission,” White said.
  > 
  > The union filed a grievance with the company, said Thomas Clouse, union president and longtime reporter. “Our understanding was that the publisher dealt with it,” he said.
  > 
  > A newsroom altercation
  > As Comma took longer than initially expected to reach its $2 million fundraising goal, the transition kept being delayed. The presence of Comma staff in the newsroom, meanwhile, grew louder.
  > 
  > Comma had also hired one of Curley’s sons. Johnathan Curley made a salary of $62,400, according to a 2025 court document, topping the pay of almost every Spokesman-Review reporter. His title at Comma is “technical director,” and he handles video and audio equipment for Comma events. Previously, the 24-year-old worked as a part-time audio-visual technician for Cirque du Soleil in Las Vegas. Johnathan Curley did not respond to requests for comment.
  > 
  > Staffers questioned how much Sylvester and Wares were getting paid to be Comma editors before the transition, and they were concerned that Wares spent half her time in California, flying back and forth on Comma’s dime.
  > 
  > Sylvester lingered in the newsroom, with staff increasingly unclear of his role. Employees described Sylvester as a disorganized and unproductive editor who often shopped online for magician supplies while in the office. He was tasked with managing the summer internship program, they said, but his involvement was so marginal that other staff had to pick up his slack.
  > 
  > “If you ask what his job entailed, I wouldn’t be able to give you an answer,” one staffer said. Sylvester did not respond to requests for comment.
  > 
  > In early August, with the planned transition to Comma just weeks away, the newsroom scrambled to cover wildfires that had caught national attention. The staff worked day and night, eventually breaking the news that one of the blazes was set by an arsonist. Courts reporter Alexandra Duggan, as it turned out, had been one of the first people to see the alleged arsonist near the flames.
  > 
  > The Review Building, which houses the Spokesman-Review office, in downtown Spokane, Washington on September 30, 2026. (Wilson Criscione/InvestigateWest)
  > 
  > On Aug. 4, Sylvester told reporters they should change how they attribute documents in stories. Instead of citing “court documents,” he said they should cite “sworn affidavits.” Despite having no clear editorial role in the Spokesman-Review newsroom, Sylvester went into Duggan’s story that night and made a change reflecting his preference — except in that instance, the record was not in fact an affidavit. It introduced an error into the paper.
  > 
  > Clouse, the union president, said he called a meeting with management to try to understand why Comma editors were meddling with stories again. “That never got resolved,” he said.
  > 
  > Two days later, Duggan, who’d worked 13 straight days, was finishing up another story about the accused arsonist. Sylvester approached her and said, in a low voice, “you’re doing it again,” several employees who witnessed or learned of the altercation told InvestigateWest and RANGE. He’d been referring to her story saying “court documents.”
  > 
  > Duggan walked away. But Sylvester followed, and the two shouted back and forth at each other until other editors stepped in to cool things down.
  > 
  > “I wanted to make sure that our stories were truthful and honest and that they were not being edited without us knowing and without any communication,” Duggan said. “And I just wanted to keep the integrity of the paper.”
  > 
  > Curley sided with Sylvester, telling Spokesman-Review managing editor Lindsey Treffry that night to fire Duggan.
  > 
  > Treffry refused.
  > 
  > The next morning, during a series of meetings with Cowles, Treffry and other editors shared complaints they had about Curley. Cowles decided to put Curley on administrative leave, revoking his keycard access to the paper’s offices.
  > 
  > A few hours later, however, Curley got in the newsroom again by asking an unsuspecting intern for their keycard. He walked around asking where he could find Treffry, who, according to several staffers there that day, was hiding from Curley in the photo room with other editors. Duggan joined them, and it was there that Duggan learned Treffry saved her job. As they hid, Duggan texted White, who was not in the newsroom at the time. White shared the texts with InvestigateWest and RANGE that described how an HR employee escorted Curley out of the building.
  > 
  > “They chased Rob out of the newsroom,” Duggan texted. “I can’t see because I’m hiding.”
  > 
  > “I’m hysterical,” Duggan wrote, crumpled into a ball and crying.
  > 
  > Duggan said she’d “ride or die for this paper.” She said Curley’s attempt to fire her “felt like a slap in the face — it felt like betrayal.”
  > 
  > In an interview for this story, she said she’ll be grateful for the rest of her life that Treffry had her back.
  > 
  > “She did save my job. And she protected the rest of us from something similar,” Duggan said.
  > 
  > “Jessica Rabbit”
  > Former reporter Rachel Alexander, who worked at the Spokesman-Review from October 2014 to July 2018, recalled a pattern of “cronyism and ethics violations.”
  > 
  > In a letter she sent to investigators after Curley’s suspension, Alexander described a departure of women on staff and in leadership roles during Curley’s first year; editors who left meetings with him looking “shaken, exhausted and at times afraid”; and how Curley didn’t keep promises to sponsor a former staff member’s work visa if he left the union.
  > 
  > “Despite the meaningful journalism I was able to do and the presence of many talented colleagues who I greatly admire, I could not in good conscience continue working for a man who was willing to lie to a friend and jeopardize his ability to remain in the country,” Alexander wrote. “I made the decision to leave the Spokesman in 2018 primarily because of Mr. Curley’s behavior.”
  > 
  > Before leaving, Alexander noticed that she was “nearly the only woman left on the desk,” according to her letter.
  > 
  > She also told investigators that Curley seemed to primarily “take issue with or pick fights” with women in the newsroom. Instead of talking through concerns, he made “petty” decisions, like blocking reporters on Twitter, she wrote.
  > 
  > In recent years, the paper has hosted high school interns as part of Curley’s effort to make Comma into a training ground for young journalists. But the behavior of Curley and Sylvester made some employees question whether they should be responsible for mentoring young journalists.
  > 
  > One letter shared with internal investigators described how, during a 2024 barbecue for Spokesman-Review interns at Curley’s house, two teenage interns saw sexually explicit images of women on Curley’s iPad as he scrolled through his camera roll. Curley had been giving the high school girls a tour of his home and showing off his guitar collection. He was looking for song lyrics on his iPad when the inappropriate pictures flashed on his screen, according to employees who had heard of the incident from the interns.
  > 
  > Several staff members also brought concerns to HR about inappropriate comments Sylvester made around interns who were in high school and college. In a conversation with another employee about the evolution of photography — and while sitting near an 18-year-old intern — Sylvester joked that if someone wanted to send a “dick pic” back in the day, they had to wait for it to develop, but today it can be done in an instant.
  > 
  > On a different occasion, Sylvester told another employee to wait a few seconds before downloading porn, according to several employees. It was loud enough for other employees to hear the unprompted comment, including nearby interns.
  > 
  > “It was very shocking to hear someone … who was supposed to be a mentor figure say that,” a staffer who witnessed the exchange said. “It’s just not appropriate for the workplace.”
  > 
  > Current and former staff members also described a work environment where people perceived as being against Curley or who reported issues to HR were retaliated against. They felt Curley had favorites — including Sylvester and Wares — who would not face consequences for their actions.
  > 
  > After an employee reported Sylvester to HR for his comment about dick pics, a letter to investigators said Curley appeared to retaliate against those he believed had made the report. Curley excluded one of the employees from important meetings and avoided another employee and gave them “distasteful looks.”
  > 
  > Curley needed to make sure staff was “loyal” to him, employees said. One employee, who requested anonymity for fear of retaliation, said Curley and his allies tried to get them to divulge whether certain Spokesman-Review employees were loyal or not. Staffers said Curley also dangled future “executive” positions to two young women in exchange for their loyalty.
  > 
  > At least one former editorial assistant filed an HR complaint against Curley. After studying creative writing at Eastern Washington University, Brooke Lauerman, now 26, was hired in December 2024 to answer newsroom calls and emails and assist with other editorial tasks. She said Curley often treated editorial assistants “like houseplants.”
  > 
  > “He would pretend we weren’t there. He’d have conversations leaning over our desk,” she said.
  > 
  > One day about a year into the job, after most employees had left for the day, Curley told Lauerman he needed to talk to her, and brought her to an empty conference room and told her to shut the door. She thought she might be getting fired. Instead, for roughly an hour, Curley “begged” her to become his personal assistant, she recalled, saying that he needed help managing his life. He told Lauerman she was “special,” that she could be an “executive lady and make a lot of money,” and that he would be her mentor.
  > 
  > He said he once had an assistant “like Jessica Rabbit, I could send her into a group of guys and they’d all say yes to her, and I want that again,” according to the complaint Lauerman filed.
  > 
  > Lauerman felt like she was being manipulated, and she didn’t understand why Curley was flattering her so much to push her into a job that had never been discussed before.
  > 
  > She made a report with HR, at the direction of her supervisor. Lauerman remembers the HR employee saying they’d “add it to my list” of complaints about Curley, and Lauerman requested that the complaint be kept quiet until she left the paper months later. She followed up with a letter detailing the experience in writing.
  > 
  > Lauerman said Curley avoided her in the months that followed. In March 2026, as her last day of work at the Spokesman approached, he asked her out to lunch, which Lauerman declined.
  > 
  > The Imagineer
  > Shortly after Curley’s suspension was announced, the union held a vote of “no confidence” in Curley. Nearly 90% of the union’s 25 members participated, and all voted in favor of the measure, other than two who abstained.
  > 
  > “He was threatening the jobs of [union] members, and we would strongly object if he were to come back to this newsroom,” Clouse said.
  > 
  > Reporters said that since Curley’s suspension, they’ve been able to focus on journalism. The newsroom “has really felt more like home than it has for a long time,” Duggan said.
  > 
  > Mathew Callaghan, a Comma reporter who contributes to the Spokesman-Review, echoed similar sentiments. He called the newsroom’s editors, reporters and those on the copy desk “some of the best, most hardworking people” he knows, but expressed some concerns over Curley.
  > 
  > “The idea of Comma is a great idea, but I don’t trust Rob Curley to execute that idea,” he said. “I don’t think that he has the best interest of the journalists that he’s supposed to uplift. … I don’t trust him and I question his ethics.”
  > 
  > The publisher has told newsroom employees he will not release results of the investigation. The union believes it’s important for the community to know what the investigation found, Clouse said.
  > 
  > An October 1, 2026 Instagram video from the Comma Community Journalism Lab account recaps a September 23 Northwest Passages event in which Spokesman-Review executive editor Rob Curley, right, interviewed former Disney Imagineer Joe Rohde, left. (Instagram)
  > 
  > It’s unclear what role Curley will continue to play at the Spokesman-Review or at Comma. The result of the pending investigation could decide his fate at the Spokesman-Review. But he may still remain the executive director of Comma, where employees may work for him again anyway. George Brunt, a Comma board member and in-house legal counsel, said that the board will honor the results of the investigation if it finds something indicating it is inappropriate for Curley to be in the newsroom, “with the caveat that the methodology was sound and not biased.” Brunt added that from what he’s seen so far, “I don’t think it was done in good faith.”
  > 
  > Last week, Curley was set to host a Comma event featuring Joe Rohde, a former Disney Imagineer. When the event started, Wares was hosting instead, but shortly into the conversation, Wares invited Curley up to the stage to conduct the interview.
  > 
  > “I’d like to invite the biggest Disney fan that I know, the person who is actually the chief Imagineer of Comma, to join me on stage. Please welcome Rob Curley,” she said.
  > 
  > The audience inside the packed theater cheered, with about a dozen people sitting near the front giving him a standing ovation, including at least one Comma employee.
  > 
  > Curley rushed onto the stage, with Elton John’s “I’m Still Standing” booming through the Gonzaga auditorium.
  > 
  > This report was produced in a collaboration between InvestigateWest and RANGE and is republished by permission.
  > 
  > Wilson Criscione is an investigations editor with InvestigateWest. InvestigateWest is an independent news nonprofit dedicated to investigative journalism in the Pacific Northwest. Visit investigatewest.org/newsletters to sign up for weekly updates.
  > 
  > Daisy Zavala Magaña is a bilingual journalist in Spokane for RANGE. RANGE is a media organization for people who love the Inland Northwest and want to make it better.

### 28. Columbia Journalism School will pause its M.A. program (and launch an online degree)
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/columbia-journalism-school-will-temporarily-pause-its-m-a-program-and-launch-an-online-degree/
- 发布时间：Thu, 01 Oct 2026 19:34:07 +0000
- 全文：
  > The Columbia Journalism School will pause admissions for its Master of Arts program, dean Jelani Cobb announced on Thursday. The country’s most prestigious journalism school also announced a new online, part-time degree.
  > 
  > In a note to alumni of the M.A. program, Cobb wrote that the administration has “initiat[ed] a critical evaluation” of the M.A. curriculum. “To give this review the focus it deserves, we are temporarily pausing admissions for the upcoming admissions cycle.”
  > 
  > The school’s admissions page has been updated with a similar message.
  > 
  > Columbia will continue to offer its Master of Science programs in Journalism and Data Journalism. Current M.A. students are not affected by the pause. The estimated cost of attendance for the nine-month M.A. for the 2026-2027 year is roughly $120,000. The M.S. costs around $130,000 and the year-long M.S. in Data Journalism costs about $180,000. According to Columbia, around 80% of students receive some sort of financial aid.
  > 
  > The M.A. program is a newer degree — created in 2005 — and was designed for working journalists with three or more years of professional experience “to go deeper into a specialized subject.” The M.S. degree, described as “the foundational program of the Journalism School,” is for students with “zero to three years” of experience.
  > 
  > The school also announced it will, for the first time in its history, offer an online degree. The new online Master of Science in Journalism program will be part-time with students earning a degree over two years.
  > 
  > “This new program brings an Ivy League J-School education directly to students across the United States,” reads one post from the Columbia Journalism School on X. “With guidance from CJS’s faculty of working journalists, you can develop your reporting skills while continuing to live and work in your community.”
  > 
  > Roughly half of enrolled M.A. candidates at Columbia Journalism School are international students, Aditya Soni and Edmund Lee reported for Reuters. Uncertainty in immigration policies and visa challenges under the Trump administration caused “a sharp drop” in international applications to Columbia, according to Reuters, and contributed to the program’s pause.
  > 
  > Here’s the initial note from Cobb:
  > 
  > Dear CJS M.A. Alumni,
  > 
  > I am writing to share an update about the Master of Arts program at Columbia Journalism School.
  > 
  > As part of our ongoing commitment to keeping our graduate education timely and innovative, the administration is initiating a critical evaluation of the M.A. curriculum. To give this review the focus it deserves, we are temporarily pausing admissions for the upcoming admissions cycle.
  > 
  > We want to reassure you directly: this evaluation phase reflects our commitment to continuous excellence. It does not alter the standing, prestige, or lasting value of your CJS M.A. degree.
  > 
  > The M.A. degree is grounded in deep domain expertise, rigorous analytical reporting, and subject-matter authority—qualities media organizations and independent journalists need now more than ever. The groundbreaking work produced by our alumni network across global newsrooms continues to define the high standards of this program and reinforce the strength of your credential.
  > 
  > Periodic curricular review is a standard practice at Columbia to ensure our specialized tracks advance alongside the media landscape. Current M.A. students are completing their nine-month curriculum without interruption, receiving the full scope of concentration coursework, thesis advisement, and faculty mentorship.
  > 
  > We remain immensely proud of your achievements and the impact of your reporting worldwide. If you have questions, please contact Dr. George Miller, associate dean for academic affairs.
  > 
  > Sincerely,
  > 
  > Jelani Cobb
  > 
  > Dean & Henry R. Luce Professor of Journalism
  > 
  > This story was updated.
  > 
  > Photo of Columbia University used under a Creative Commons license.

### 29. California’s big local news bill becomes law
- 源站：Nieman Journalism Lab
- 链接：https://www.niemanlab.org/2026/10/californias-big-local-news-bill-becomes-law/
- 发布时间：Thu, 01 Oct 2026 18:44:51 +0000
- 全文：
  > At the end of August, the state of California passed a bill to give news organizations tax credits based on the number of journalists they employ.
